# Stage 1 detailed curriculum: 1.4 Linear algebra

Blueprint work file, Stage 2 Phase 1. Draft 2, 6 October 2026.

**Source of truth.** `docs/curriculum.md` for stages, topics, hours and exit tests. This file expands item 1.4 Linear algebra (150 h) into courses, levels, lessons, homework, the topic exam, the rebuilt exit test, reading and verification, and writes one lesson in full.

**Draft 2** (same day) keeps every section number, lesson number, id and level code of draft 1, because `ASSESSMENT.md`, `canon-gap.md`, `CONTENT-FORMAT.md`, `stage1-statistics-ts.md` and `stage1-python-tools.md` cite them (lessons 1, 2, 39 to 51, 53, 64, 70, 82, 86, 95 and 96; sections 4.1 and 4.3; decision 6; the request table). It reconciles this file with what was written after draft 1: `ARCHITECTURE.md` (Pyodide on both targets, MicroPython dropped, decision 15); `ASSESSMENT.md` draft 2 (the `finals` paper kind, follow-through marking, the 7-day resit, Mastered at 75%, estimates against hours); `CONTENT-FORMAT.md` revision 2 (element references, tutor fields, `needs`, the quiz-letter table that adopted this file's letter B); `canon-gap.md` revision 2 (source ids, P10 moved to 2.1); `AI-TUTOR.md` draft 2 and `BERTIE.md` (Bertie's gate and states); the draft 2 Stage 0 files and `stage1-python-tools.md` draft 2 (the final 0.2, 0.3 and 1.1 lesson lists); and `spikes/README.md`. Section 0 lists every change and its reason.

> **Revision 2 of `docs/PLAN.md`, 7 October 2026: the Artifact publishing rule** (owner correction 5; added by the plan's second review round). These rows win over anything below that reads otherwise, the earlier review's rows included; content agents are briefed from them, and CI enforces them on the lesson files.
>
> | Where | Was | Now |
> |---|---|---|
> | Draft 2 changes 1 and 2; the verification setup; 1.0 decision 10; the Runtime paragraph of section 2; 4.3 capstones; the Linear Algebra Lab; 6 | Pyodide 314.0.7 on both targets; the standard library, NumPy and pandas in the Artifact; which further packages ship in the Artifact build left open | **No Python, SQL or C++ in the Artifact.** The Artifact publishes only files built from this repository's own sources (`docs/PLAN.md` 4.3), so no Pyodide, SQLite or compiler ships there. Every drill and every code item (K items, code screens, Labs, capstones, projects) runs in Pyodide 314.0.7 on the web app, or on CPython 3.12 on the learner's machine where this file says so; in the Artifact it shows its starter code, recorded output and the web-app link, and returns by result code (`docs/ASSESSMENT.md` 14.3). A lesson screen or quiz item whose widget runs learner code resolves as `read` in the Artifact and is finished in the web app (`docs/PLAN.md` 4.3, live-code screens). Lessons are otherwise unaffected: authored code runs at build time and ships as recorded output, and every number a widget shows comes from our own JavaScript models. Maths renders as MathML in the Artifact. Wherever this file says a drill, Lab, capstone, project or paper with code runs "on both targets" or "in the Artifact", or that Pyodide, NumPy or pandas run in the Artifact, read "in the web app"; the iPhone run of Pyodide inside an Artifact (`spikes/README.md`) is a record, not adopted, and where this file calls the brief's "Pyodide cannot run in the Artifact" a conflict, the brief's outcome now holds by the publishing rule |
> | Papers and exits | Capstones, the Lab and the exam on both targets | **A paper with any code item is sat in the web app** (`docs/ASSESSMENT.md` 3.3; `docs/AI-TUTOR.md` 2.12); a paper whose items need no runtime (predicted output, recorded traces and outputs) plays on both targets. An exit part that needs a drill, Lab or capstone has that part done in the web app. The `1.4.exit` capstones and the 12 Linear Algebra Lab drills run in the web app; `exm.linalg` has no code ("Code: None") and plays on both targets |
> | CI parity | CPython against Pyodide justified by "both targets run Pyodide" | **CI parity is CPython 3.12 (`tools/py/uv.lock`) against Pyodide 314.0.7 on the web app**: Pyodide grades every drill there, and CPython 3.12 is the learner's own floor, so any difference between them fails CI. Runs recorded on CPython 3.11 stand as records and are rerun at `docs/PLAN.md` stage 26 |

**Built on.** `docs/design-research.md` sections 5, 8, 9 and 10 (lesson anatomy, curriculum architecture, teaching style, critiques); `docs/blueprint/work/widgets-calc-linalg.md` (widget ids, modes and element ids; its section 1.2 is the working outline this file replaces); `docs/CONTENT-FORMAT.md` revision 2 (ids, schema, screen types, checks, tutor fields, element references, drills, verification); `docs/ASSESSMENT.md` draft 2 (assessment ladder, the `finals` paper kind, the exit row `1.4.exit`, earned hours); `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` (decision 15 and section 6.3, Python on both targets); `docs/AI-TUTOR.md` draft 2 (Bertie's gate and marking); `docs/BERTIE.md` (states and who drives them); `docs/blueprint/work/canon-gap.md` revision 2 (the 1.4 resource canon, its `src.*` ids and licences, proposal P10); the sibling files `docs/curriculum/stage0-number-algebra.md` and `docs/curriculum/stage0-precalc-computing.md` draft 2 (the 0.2 and 0.3 lessons this item extends and does not repeat), `docs/curriculum/stage1-python-tools.md` draft 2 (NumPy, `@`, `solve`, the runtime differences) and `docs/curriculum/stage1-calculus.md` (gradients, the Hessian, least squares by calculus, Lagrange multipliers); `spikes/README.md`; the owner's lesson-creator, maths-teaching, code-craft and project-forge skills.

**Tags.** Decisions sit in tables with the rejected alternative and the reason. [verified] marks a number recomputed on 6 October 2026: exact claims with Python 3.11.15 and SymPy 1.14.0, floating-point claims with NumPy 2.4.6 on both CPython 3.11.15 and Pyodide 314.0.7 (Python 3.14.2), the app's runtime on both targets. Every numeric answer in sections 2 (hooks, traps and edges that state a number), 3, 4 and 7 is [verified]; the tag is not repeated on each. [measured] marks a runtime behaviour or timing measured while writing this draft (section 0.2). [searched] marks a resource detail confirmed by a web search on 6 October 2026; math.mit.edu, ocw.mit.edu, ams.org and epubs.siam.org were blocked by this environment's proxy, so searched details rest on search summaries, not on the pages themselves. [check] marks a resource detail (a section, lecture, chapter or problem number, a URL path, a licence) recalled but not confirmed; confirm it before content cites it (`checked: false` in `Level.companion` until then). [recalled] marks a fact from memory that I am confident of. [our choice] marks a design decision without outside evidence. [mode to add] marks a widget mode or option the catalogue does not yet list. [unverified] marks a platform fact not yet measured.

| 1 | **Python runs on Pyodide** (on the web app only since revision 2: header row). Draft 1 tagged drills `micropython-ok`, `ulab-ok` or `web` and sent every NumPy drill from the Artifact to the web app. Draft 2 drops the tags: every drill declares `needs`, and 1.4 needs only `[]` (the standard library with `fractions`) or `[numpy]`, both of which the Artifact ships [sourced: the owner's iPhone, `spikes/README.md`]. No 1.4 item needs SciPy; draft 1's one SciPy drill (`lab-lu-timing`) becomes an operation count (`lab-lu-cost`). So every lesson, homework drill, capstone, Lab drill and exam runs on both targets, and draft 1's open items 5 and 6 are closed | 1.0 decision 10, 1.5, 2.0, 3.0 to 3.6, 4.3, 6 | `ARCHITECTURE.md` decision 15; `ASSESSMENT.md` decision 30; `CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.2 (`Drill.needs`) |
| 2 | **A conflict with the brief, resolved by measurement.** The brief says Pyodide cannot run in the Artifact and that WebAssembly there was still being measured. The spikes measured both on the owner's iPhone: every probe passed except service workers, and a repackaged Pyodide loaded NumPy and pandas and computed correctly. This file follows the measurement, as every sibling paper now does. The brief's second outcome stays covered: a device that cannot load Python hands an item to the web app and takes back a result code (`ASSESSMENT.md` 14.3) | 0.2, 1.0 decision 10 | `spikes/README.md`; `CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 0.3; `ASSESSMENT.md` 0.4 |
| 3 | **Four NumPy differences that matter for linear algebra**, found by running every floating-point claim on both runtimes (section 0.2). (a) `np.linalg.solve`, `inv` and `cholesky` return arrays of `nan` in the app where CPython raises `LinAlgError`, for singular, indefinite and even positive semidefinite input. (b) A matrix that is singular only in exact arithmetic raises nothing on either runtime: the normal equations of the dummy-variable trap and of a pounds-and-pence regression return finite coefficients that differ between the two runtimes (for the same data, a pounds coefficient of 0.33 on CPython and $-0.40$ in the app), while `lstsq` and the fitted values agree. (c) Digits lost to ill-conditioning are noise, and the noise differs: the degree-10 Vandermonde normal equations miss by $2.1\times 10^{-3}$ on CPython and $2.4\times 10^{-2}$ in the app. (d) `solve`'s timing exponent between $n = 200$, 400 and 800 was 1.4 and 2.6 on CPython with this environment's multithreaded BLAS and 2.0 and 3.0 in the app. Lessons 31, 45, 48, 79 and 81 now teach (a) to (c) as edge cases; no item depends on an exception, a warning or a timing (decision 15) | 1.0 decision 15, 2.2, 2.3, 2.5, 3, 4.3, 6 | [measured] |
| 4 | **Errors in draft 1 found by running it.** Lesson 45's hook said "the solver refuses" and lesson 31's that the regression "will not solve": neither raises on either runtime (change 3b). Lesson 48 quoted CPython's error as if it were the error. Homework Z3 example B called the hedged book "a short-wings, long-belly butterfly" and missed most of its remaining exposure: $\sqrt{20}\approx 4.47$ along the fourth direction, against $-2$ on curvature. Section 2.0 gave the namespaces `elimination` and `svd` the letters M and X, which the course codes had already replaced with V and Z. The exit's `estHours` of 13 counted `exm.linalg`'s 3 hours a second time (the paper's minutes already carry them): now 11. The exam table allowed a resit after 3 days; finals-style papers wait 7. Section 4.1 promised two practice papers and the hours table paid for one. The Why? on sample screen 7 ran to five sentences against design research 9.4's three. Lab `lab-image-svd` shipped a `.png`, which needs an image library the app does not load: it ships a `.json` array. The pace of 12 weeks left out 1.7 Finance's share of the Main block: about 14 weeks | 2.0, 2.2, 2.3, 3.5, 3.6, 4.1, 4.3, 7 | [measured]; `ASSESSMENT.md` 0.3 and 3.8; `CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.3 |
| 5 | **Overlap with Stage 0, 1.1 and 1.3 removed.** The final lesson lists, written after draft 1, already teach elimination on $3\times 3$ systems and the three cases for three planes (0.2 lesson 53), two-state replication (0.2 lesson 54, 0.3 lesson 92), the dot product with the cosine rule, projection, cosine similarity and correlation as a cosine of centred data (0.3 lessons 37 and 38), $AB\ne BA$ and $AB = 0$ (0.3 lesson 88), images of basis vectors as columns and translations as non-linear (0.3 lesson 89), composition order (0.3 lesson 90), $ad - bc$ with the "negative area" trap (0.3 lesson 91), $(AB)^{-1} = B^{-1}A^{-1}$ (0.3 lesson 92), `@`, `solve` and the app's `nan` (1.1 lesson 67), gradient descent in a narrow valley (1.3 lesson 117) and the normal equations of a line with slope $\operatorname{cov}/\operatorname{var}$ (1.3 lesson 122). Lessons 3, 7, 9, 11, 17, 39, 54 and 96 get a new hook or trap and lessons 6, 18, 41 and 43 say what they extend, so that 1.4 builds on earlier teaching and never repeats it (section 1.0, second table). Lesson numbers and ids are unchanged | 1.0, 2.1 to 2.5, 7 | `stage0-number-algebra.md` 2 (0.2 lessons 49 to 54); `stage0-precalc-computing.md` 2.1 (0.3 lessons 33 to 38, 87 to 97); `stage1-python-tools.md` 2.1 (lesson 67); `stage1-calculus.md` 2 (lessons 117, 122) |

### 0.2 What was executed

All on 6 October 2026, in a scratch folder outside the repository [measured]:

- **Two Python runtimes.** CPython 3.11.15 with NumPy 2.4.6, SciPy 1.17.1 and SymPy 1.14.0 (the learner's floor and CI's verifier), and Pyodide 314.0.7 (Python 3.14.2, NumPy 2.4.6), the web app's runtime (revision 2 header row), loaded in Node 22.22 from the `pyodide` npm package with the spike's NumPy wheel.
- **Exact claims.** One script of 87 assertions recomputed in exact arithmetic every number that a hook, trap or edge in section 2 states, every homework example answer and distractor in section 3, the exam specimen in section 4.2, and every answer and diagnosed wrong answer of the sample lesson and its quiz. All 87 pass. It found the Z3 example B omission (change 4) by checking the hedged book against all four loadings, not three.
- **Floating-point claims.** One script ran unchanged on both runtimes: lessons 16 (no pivoting), 48 (normal equations against `lstsq` and QR), 52 (classical and modified Gram-Schmidt and Householder on the Läuchli matrix), 88 (the condition number), 93 and 95 (noise eigenvalues, seeded), 96 (step sizes), 98 (Wilkinson's polynomial), the Lab drills (Hilbert conditioning, orthogonality loss, `solve` against `inv`), the dummy-variable and pounds-and-pence regressions, NumPy on singular and indefinite matrices, and the exit capstone's Householder QR at $m = 10{,}000$, $n = 50$ (0.23 s on CPython and 0.25 s on Pyodide in Node). Where the two runtimes differ, section 2 quotes what they share or an order of magnitude (decision 15).
- **Formulas.** Every one of the 2,422 formulas in this file renders without error in KaTeX 0.16.47 strict mode (the copy at hand; CONTENT-FORMAT.md section 0.2 used 0.19.0, which CI pins).
- **Searches.** Strang's 6th edition: the ten chapter titles; sections 1.3, 1.4, 2.5, 3.2, 4.5, 5.1 to 5.3, 6.3 to 6.5, 7.3, 8.1, 8.3, 9.1 to 9.3 and 10.3; appendices 1 to 8 (among them 5, the Jordan form, 7, the condition number, and 8, Markov matrices and Perron-Frobenius). MIT 18.06 Spring 2010: the titles of lectures 1, 2, 9, 24b, 26, 31, 33 and 34, and that the videos were recorded in 1999 and published with the 2010 course. The pages themselves were blocked, so these are [searched] from search summaries and agree with what I recalled.

The scripts are scratch checks, not repository code: `zqverify` and the drill runners replace them at build stage (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` section 6).

## 1. Item overview

### 1.0 Scope, boundaries and decisions

| # | Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Reason |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Unit of lesson time | **15 minutes per lesson, quiz included**, as in Stage 0, 1.1 and 1.3 | 10 or 12 minutes | One unit across the app keeps the hours tables comparable. Matrix entry is slower than tapping, so the upper end of design research 5.8's 10 to 15 minutes is the honest figure |
| 2 | Spine | **Strang, Introduction to Linear Algebra, 6th edition (2023)** (`src.strang-ila-6e`), with the MIT 18.06 lectures (`src.mit-1806`), as the curriculum names; 3Blue1Brown's Essence of Linear Algebra watched first, as the curriculum says | Axler; Boyd and Vandenberghe as spine; Strang 5th edition | The curriculum's pick and its exit (the 18.06 final) both point at Strang. Canon-gap pins the 6th edition, whose ten chapter titles are [searched]; section 5 also maps every reading to the 5th edition, which many learners own |
| 3 | Number of courses | **Five**: V Vectors, Matrices and Elimination (18 lessons), N Spaces, Rank and the Four Subspaces (19), O Orthogonality and Least Squares (16), H Hidden Directions: Determinants and Eigenvalues (23), Z Data Matrices: Positive Definiteness, the SVD and PCA (22) | Seven courses, one per Strang chapter (the widget catalogue's working outline); three large courses | Five is the task's ceiling and gives each course one story with a capstone level. Strang's chapters 5 and 6 (determinants, eigenvalues) are one story: the determinant exists here to find eigenvalues |
| 4 | Order | **Strang's order**: combinations, elimination, spaces, orthogonality, determinants, eigenvalues, positive definiteness, SVD, PCA. One change: linear maps (lesson 17) come in course V, not after the SVD | Determinants first (the British A-level order); eigenvalues before orthogonality | Elimination is the engine every later topic runs on, and the 18.06 final follows this order. 0.3 already taught $2\times 2$ transformations (lessons 89 to 92), so the transformation view can start at once; Strang's chapter 8 returns in level N4 with bases |
| 5 | What is added beyond the cover list | **A top-programme layer**: $PA = LU$ and partial pivoting, block matrices and the Schur complement, replication and state prices, graphs and incidence matrices, change of basis and similarity, the Jordan form (stated), Frisch-Waugh-Lovell, weighted and ridge least squares, Householder reflections, Fourier series and the FFT, complex and Hermitian matrices, Markov chains and PageRank, $e^{At}$ and stability, vector autoregressions, Cholesky, valid correlation matrices, gradients of quadratic forms, the pseudoinverse, norms and conditioning, Marchenko-Pastur noise, covariance shrinkage, graph Laplacians, how eigenvalues are computed | The cover list only (elimination, spaces, rank, orthogonality, projections, least squares, determinants, eigenvalues, SVD, positive definite matrices) | The 18.06 syllabus already contains most of these (Markov, FFT, $e^{At}$, Jordan, graphs, pseudoinverse); the rest are what quant and ML-lab work uses daily: conditioning (why `solve` and QR beat the normal equations), Cholesky (correlated simulation), shrinkage (every covariance a desk uses), ridge (2.1). Each addition outside the 18.06 syllabus and the exit is one lesson; together they are 26 lessons (3, 15, 16, 18, 36, 37, 43 to 48, 52, 59, 67, 70, 74, 79 to 82, 88, 95 to 98), about 6.5 hours |
| 6 | Matrix calculus (canon-gap P10) | **Core inside 1.4's 150 hours**: lesson 82 (gradients of linear and quadratic forms, least squares and ridge by calculus, finite-difference checks), lesson 86 (Eckart-Young) and lesson 96 (gradient descent on a quadratic). The remainder of P10 (+10 h: Jacobians and the matrix chain rule, softmax and logistic gradients, backpropagation through a two-layer network, $\nabla_X\operatorname{tr}(AX)$ and $\nabla_X\log\det X$) opens 2.1, which canon-gap revision 2 adopted | All of P10 inside 1.4; none of it | The normal equations and ridge are 1.4 results, and lesson 82 derives them a second way in a few lines. Backpropagation belongs with 2.1, where its use is; 1.3 lesson 116 already teaches it as the chain rule on a graph of scalars |
| 7 | Arithmetic | **Exact rational arithmetic** wherever the learner computes by hand (elimination, RREF, inverses, determinants, nullspaces, exact eigenvalues); **floats** in the numerical lessons (16, 48, 52, 88, 94 to 98) and every NumPy drill | Floats everywhere | Widget catalogue decision 3. $\tfrac13$ must never display as 0.3333 in a pivot, and a lesson about round-off must show real round-off |
| 8 | Proof | **Proof at the 18.06 standard, not Axler's**: every level's homework has a long-form proof or derivation; the topic exam gives at least 40% of its marks to proofs, derivations and explanations. Proofs build on 0.3 levels R1 and R2 (logic, contradiction, induction) | No proofs (a computational course); Axler-style abstract proofs | The exit paper is an 18.06 final, which asks for short proofs and justified true-or-false answers. Axler is the curriculum's "not yet" |
| 9 | Notation | **Strang's notation**, with three fixes. (a) The eigenvector matrix is $X$ ($A = X\Lambda X^{-1}$), as in the 6th edition; the recorded lectures write $S$, and lessons say so once. (b) $S$ is reserved for symmetric matrices and the sample covariance; $\Sigma$ for a true covariance matrix in finance lessons and for the singular-value matrix in SVD lessons; in the three lessons where both appear (89, 93, 94) the covariance is $S$ and the SVD diagonal is $\Sigma$. (c) Markov matrices follow Strang (columns add to 1, $\mathbf u_{k+1} = A\mathbf u_k$) in 1.4, and lesson 66 converts explicitly to the row convention that 1.5 and Blitzstein and Hwang use | Mixed notation per source | One symbol, one meaning, inside the app; the conversions are taught where a learner meets the other convention |
| 10 | Code and runtimes | **Pyodide on both targets; every drill declares `needs`.** Drills written in pure Python declare `needs: []`: exact `Fraction`s for elimination, $LU$, RREF, the complete solution, the four subspaces, the derivative matrix, determinants and matrix powers, and `math` floats for the power method and Cholesky by hand. The rest declare `needs: [numpy]`. No 1.4 item needs SciPy, statsmodels or scikit-learn (SciPy appears only in CI's verifier). Lesson screens run on both targets with Python never loaded, because authored code runs in CI and ships as recorded output; below 768 px every code screen also offers its Parsons version | Draft 1's runtime tags (`micropython-ok`, `ulab-ok`, `web`), with NumPy drills handed off from the Artifact; NumPy everywhere, including the exact-arithmetic drills | `ARCHITECTURE.md` decision 15 and `ASSESSMENT.md` decision 30: NumPy runs inside the Artifact [sourced: `spikes/README.md`], so 1.4 hands nothing off. Exact drills stay pure Python because `Fraction` keeps pivots exact and the code is the algorithm, not a library call |
| 11 | Quant and ML transfer | **One transfer screen per lesson where a true finance or ML context exists**, at least one per level; levels O2, Z3 and Z4 are built around such contexts | A finance context forced into every lesson | As Stage 0 decision 10. Linear algebra has honest contexts everywhere (exposures, replication, arbitrage loops, betas, factor neutrality, covariance, PCA of curves and returns), and forced ones teach nothing |
| 12 | Exam | **`exm.linalg`, kind `finals`** (`ASSESSMENT.md` 3.1): 180 minutes, 100 marks, 10 multi-part questions, no calculator, exact answers, no code, follow-through declared per part; sat on either target; resit after 7 days and a remediation set | A standard 90-minute topic paper | `ASSESSMENT.md` assigns the `finals` kind to 1.3, 1.4 and 1.5 because their exits are university finals |
| 13 | Exit rebuild | **Gate**: `exm.linalg` at 70% and two capstone drills (least squares by the normal equations through the learner's Cholesky and by the learner's Householder QR; PCA by the SVD). **Confirm**: one real 18.06 final, timed at 3 hours, self-marked at 70%. `estHours` 11 | In-app only; real paper only | `ASSESSMENT.md` decision 1 and row `1.4.exit` |
| 14 | Sample lesson | **Lesson 4, "$A\mathbf x$ is a combination of columns"** | Lesson 1 (linear combinations); lesson 7 (elimination) | It is the root of the item's dependency graph: the column space, solvability, rank, least squares (project $\mathbf b$ onto the columns) and the SVD ($A\mathbf v = \sigma\mathbf u$) all read $A\mathbf x$ as weights on columns. It is also where 0.3's row-times-column habit causes the item's most common error ($A^\top\mathbf x$ for $A\mathbf x$), so it carries the most important trap |
| 15 | Floating-point items (new in draft 2) | **Every floating-point claim is recorded on both runtimes; an item quotes what both agree on to its stated precision, or an order of magnitude; no item, hidden test or screen depends on a `LinAlgError`, a NumPy warning or a timing.** Code that must detect a singular or indefinite matrix tests rank, finiteness or the smallest eigenvalue, and the lessons that meet the difference teach it | Quoting CPython's digits; teaching "NumPy raises `LinAlgError`" as a fact | In the app NumPy has no floating-point flags and its `linalg` returns `nan` where CPython raises, and digits lost to ill-conditioning differ between the two [measured, section 0.2]. A learner moves between the app and their own machine, so both behaviours are true for them |

**What 1.4 leaves to other items.**

| Topic | Where it is taught | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Vectors in 2D and 3D, the dot product, $2\times 2$ matrices, transformations, $2\times 2$ determinants and inverses | 0.3 levels S8 and T5 (lessons 33 to 38, 87 to 92) | 1.4 recaps them only inside hooks and extends them to $n$ dimensions (second table) |
| Gradients, Hessians, gradient descent, Lagrange multipliers, least squares as minimisation | 1.3 lessons 106 to 128 (functions of several variables 106 to 108, partial derivatives 109, the gradient 114, backpropagation 116, gradient descent 117, the Hessian 119 and 120, least squares 122, Lagrange multipliers 124 to 128) | Lessons 41, 78, 80, 82 and 96 cite them; 1.4 adds the matrix view |
| The multivariate normal, conditional Gaussians (the Schur complement in use), Markov chains in probability | 1.5 | Lessons 15, 59, 66 and 81 plant the linear algebra |
| Regression inference: standard errors, $t$ and $F$ statistics, $R^2$, the statsmodels table | 1.6 | Lessons 43 to 48 do the fitting; 1.6 does the inference (its exit) |
| Proof-based linear algebra (Axler), abstract vector spaces in depth | Canon-gap P7 (1.4b) and later stretch | The curriculum's "not yet" |
| Numerical linear algebra in depth: backward stability, Krylov and iterative methods, sparse matrices | Canon-gap P3 (Stage 2, Trefethen and Bau) | Lessons 16, 48, 52, 88 and 98 give the working ideas |
| Linear programming, duality, convex optimisation | Canon-gap P4 (Stage 2, Boyd and Vandenberghe) | Strang 6th edition 9.4 is optional reading [check its title] |
| Backpropagation in matrix form, Jacobians of networks, the rest of P10 | The opening of 2.1 (canon-gap revision 2, +10 h) | Decision 6 |
| Estimating vector autoregressions; cointegration | 2.2 | Lesson 70 gives the stability test and the unit root |
| Tensors, Kronecker products, the Jordan form's proof, Perron-Frobenius's proof | Not covered | Stated where used (lessons 66, 71); Strang's appendices 5, 6 and 8 hold them [searched] |

**What 1.4 takes from earlier items and does not repeat** (new in draft 2; change 5).

| Earlier teaching | Where | What 1.4 does with it |
|---|---|---|
| $2\times 2$ and $3\times 3$ systems by elimination and back substitution; three planes meet in a point, along a line or not at all | 0.2 lessons 49 to 53 | Lesson 6 adds the column picture and tests the belief that non-parallel planes must meet; lesson 7 turns elimination into an algorithm on numbers alone (the augmented matrix, pivots, multipliers, a fixed column order) with a $4\times 4$ hook |
| Replicating a two-state payoff with a share and a bond | 0.2 lesson 54; 0.3 lesson 92 | Lesson 18 goes to three states, state prices, arbitrage and incomplete markets |
| Vectors, the dot product from the cosine rule, projection onto a vector, 3D, cosine similarity, correlation as the cosine of centred data | 0.3 lessons 33 to 38 (lesson 37's transfer screen) | Lesson 3 proves Cauchy-Schwarz in $\mathbb R^n$, where no triangle can be drawn, and traps "correlation is transitive"; lesson 39 builds the projection matrix |
| Matrices as arrays, row-by-column multiplication, $AB\ne BA$, $AB = 0$ with neither factor zero | 0.3 lessons 87 and 88 | Lesson 4 reads $A\mathbf x$ by columns; lesson 9 multiplies four ways, traps $(A + B)^2$ and prices the order of a product |
| The images of $(1, 0)$ and $(0, 1)$ are the columns; a translation is not a matrix map | 0.3 lesson 89 | Lesson 17 goes between spaces of different dimensions and traps "$T(\mathbf 0) = \mathbf 0$, so $T$ is linear" |
| Composition order ($BA$ means $A$ first) | 0.3 lesson 90 | Lesson 10 orders elimination matrices |
| $ad - bc$ as the signed area factor; a negative determinant flips orientation | 0.3 lesson 91 | Lesson 54 starts at $3\times 3$ volume and traps "no two columns parallel, so not singular" |
| The $2\times 2$ inverse; $(AB)^{-1} = B^{-1}A^{-1}$ | 0.3 lesson 92 | Lesson 11 does Gauss-Jordan for $n\times n$, proves the rule in one line and traps reading the inverse off halfway |
| Complex numbers in polar form, roots of unity, Euler's formula previewed | 0.3 lessons 93 to 97; 1.3 lesson 104 | Lessons 63, 75 and 76 |
| `@` against `*`, `solve` rather than `inv`, the app's `nan` for a singular matrix | 1.1 lesson 67 | Lesson 4 adds which side of `@` the vector sits on; lessons 16, 45 and 48 explain why `solve` and QR are better; lessons 79 and 81 meet the `nan` again for Cholesky |
| Gradient descent by trial in a long narrow valley | 1.3 lesson 117 | Lesson 96 predicts the largest and the best step from the eigenvalues |
| The normal equations of a line by calculus; slope $= \operatorname{cov}/\operatorname{var}$ | 1.3 lesson 122 | Lesson 41 derives them by geometry; lesson 43 reads beta as a projection coefficient |
| The Hessian and the second derivative test | 1.3 lessons 119 and 120 | Lessons 77 and 78 generalise to $n\times n$ |
| The minimum-variance portfolio of uncorrelated assets | 1.3 lesson 128 | Lesson 80 does correlated assets |
| The Jacobian in a change of variables | 1.3 lesson 134 | Lesson 59 is its linear case |

### 1.1 Purpose in a quant or ML career

- **It is the language of every model a desk or lab runs.** A regression is a projection, a factor model is a low-rank matrix, a covariance matrix is a positive semidefinite quadratic form, PCA is an SVD, a neural network layer is $A\mathbf x + \mathbf b$ followed by a nonlinearity. A researcher who cannot see these shapes cannot debug the model.
- **Interviews test it directly and indirectly.** Quant interviews ask for eigenvalues of a $2\times 2$ matrix in the head, whether a proposed correlation matrix is valid (Zhou's green book has this problem class [check section]), the minimum-variance portfolio, and why a regression with collinear features fails. ML research interviews ask for the SVD, PCA, the normal equations and the condition number.
- **The expensive bugs are linear-algebra bugs.** Inverting a near-singular covariance matrix gives portfolio weights that swing wildly from day to day; fitting by the normal equations loses half the digits; forgetting to centre data before PCA puts the first component on the mean; the dummy-variable trap silently breaks a sector model and, as draft 2 measured, returns different coefficients on different machines without an error; an invalid correlation matrix makes a Cholesky-based simulator fail or, worse, run on a repaired matrix nobody checked. Each of these is a trap screen in this item.
- **It makes the next items possible.** 1.5 needs eigenvectors for Markov chains and covariance matrices for joint distributions; 1.6's exit is regression in NumPy; 2.1 is mostly linear models, regularisation and PCA; 2.2 needs eigenvalues for stability and cointegration; Stage 3 papers (factor models, pairs trading, Avellaneda and Stoikov) assume all of it.
- **Computation is part of the subject.** A quant computes with floating point every day. Cost ($n^3/3$), pivoting, orthogonal methods and conditioning are taught as part of linear algebra, not as an afterthought.

### 1.2 Learning outcomes

At the end of 1.4 the learner can:

| # | Outcome | Measured by |
|---|---|---|
| LA-O1 | Compute and interpret linear combinations, spans, dot products and $A\mathbf x$, reading $A\mathbf x$ both as a combination of columns and as dot products with rows | 85% on fresh difficulty-3 items; exam Q1 |
| LA-O2 | Solve $n\times n$ systems by elimination in exact arithmetic, factor $A = LU$ and $PA = LU$, compute inverses by Gauss-Jordan, and say when and why elimination fails | 85% on difficulty-3 items; exam Q1 |
| LA-O3 | State the cost of elimination ($n^3/3$) and of each extra right-hand side ($n^2$), explain partial pivoting with a numerical example, choose `solve` or a factorisation over an explicit inverse, and detect a singular or indefinite matrix by rank, finiteness or eigenvalues rather than by waiting for an exception | Homework V3 long-form at 7 of 10 |
| LA-O4 | Decide whether a set is a subspace; compute bases and dimensions of the four fundamental subspaces and the complete solution of $A\mathbf x = \mathbf b$, with the solvability conditions on $\mathbf b$ | 85% on difficulty-3 items; exam Q2 |
| LA-O5 | Prove the standard results at the 18.06 standard: Cauchy-Schwarz, uniqueness of dimension, rank-nullity, $N(A^\top A) = N(A)$, $\det(AB) = \det A\det B$, independence of eigenvectors for distinct eigenvalues, real eigenvalues and orthogonal eigenvectors of symmetric matrices, the energy test for positive definiteness | Homework long-form items at 7 of 10; exam Q3 and Q10 |
| LA-O6 | Project onto lines and subspaces, derive and solve the normal equations, fit lines, polynomials and multi-factor regressions, and use the orthogonality of residuals | 85% on difficulty-3 items; exam Q4 |
| LA-O7 | Orthonormalise by Gram-Schmidt, factor $A = QR$, solve least squares by QR, and explain why modified Gram-Schmidt and Householder are stable where classical Gram-Schmidt and the normal equations are not | Exam Q5; capstone `drl.linalg.exit-lstsq` |
| LA-O8 | Compute determinants by elimination and by cofactors, use their properties, and interpret them as signed volume and as the density factor under a linear change of variables | Exam Q6 |
| LA-O9 | Find eigenvalues and eigenvectors (including complex and repeated cases), diagonalise, compute $A^k$ and $e^{At}$, find Markov steady states, and classify the stability of discrete and continuous systems | 85% on difficulty-3 items; exam Q7 |
| LA-O10 | Use the spectral theorem; test positive definiteness five ways; validate covariance and correlation matrices; factor by Cholesky and simulate correlated normals | Exam Q8; homework Z1 |
| LA-O11 | Compute and interpret the SVD, best low-rank approximations, the pseudoinverse, matrix norms and condition numbers | Exam Q9; homework Z2 |
| LA-O12 | Carry out PCA correctly (centring, scaling, explained variance, sign convention) and interpret loadings of yield-curve changes and of equity returns, including a noise floor | Homework Z3; capstone `drl.linalg.exit-pca` |
| LA-O13 | Apply linear algebra to finance: replication and state prices, arbitrage loops, factor exposures and neutral portfolios, CAPM and multi-factor regression, minimum-variance portfolios, covariance shrinkage | Transfer screens; homework V3, N3, N4, O2, Z1, Z4; the Factor Risk Lab project |
| LA-O14 | Implement least squares (normal equations and QR) and PCA (SVD) in NumPy from matrix operations, with tests, on either target | `1.4.exit` capstone drills |
| LA-O15 | Pass an 18.06-style final at 70% | `1.4.exit`: `exm.linalg` and one real 18.06 final |

### 1.3 Prerequisites

- **0.2** (Algebra): simultaneous equations and $3\times 3$ elimination (lessons 49 to 53), replication of a two-state payoff (lesson 54), functions, polynomials and their roots (characteristic polynomials), quadratics (eigenvalues of $2\times 2$ matrices).
- **0.3** (Precalculus): level S8, vectors in 2D and 3D, the dot product, projections and cosine similarity (lessons 33 to 38); level T5, $2\times 2$ matrices, multiplication, transformations, rotation matrices, determinants as area factors, inverses (lessons 87 to 92); level T6, complex numbers in polar form, De Moivre and roots of unity (lessons 93 to 97, for 1.4 lessons 63, 75 and 76); levels R1 and R2, logic, contradiction and induction (every proof); level C1, permutations (the big formula, lesson 57); level G5, series (the power series of $e^{At}$).
- **1.1** (Python): NumPy arrays, `@` against `*`, `solve` against `inv` and the app's `nan` for a singular matrix (lesson 67 there), floating point in arrays (lesson 66), seeded simulation (level A3), matplotlib. Every NumPy drill here assumes them.
- **1.2** (Tools): Git and pytest. The capstone drills and the Factor Risk Lab project live in the learner's repository with tests.
- **1.3** (Calculus): Taylor series and Euler's formula (1.3 lessons 99 to 104, for $e^{At}$ and complex eigenvalues); first-order differential equations and mean reversion (1.3 lessons 82 to 85, for lesson 68); partial derivatives, gradients, backpropagation as the chain rule on a graph, gradient descent, the Hessian test and least squares as minimisation (1.3 lessons 109 to 122, for lessons 41, 78, 82, 96); the Jacobian in change of variables (1.3 lesson 134, for lesson 59); Lagrange multipliers and the minimum-variance portfolio (1.3 lessons 124 to 128, for lesson 80). Lesson numbers are those of `docs/curriculum/stage1-calculus.md` and were rechecked against it for draft 2.
- 0.5's Daily 10 continues. The weekly probability problems start at 1.5, not here.

Practice sets: `set.<course>.<level>-practice`, kind `practice`. Redo: spent time, no estimate |

Reading 5.75 h: Strang's sections per level, VMLS and ESL for level O2, MML and Litterman and Scheinkman for Z3, Ledoit and Wolf for Z4. External problems 10.75 h: Strang problem sets, the three timed 18.06 Spring 2010 unit exams, Zhou's linear algebra problems and VMLS exercises, as listed per level in section 5 | 35.5 | 23.7% | `Level.companion` hours (section 5) |
| Projects and drills | Exit capstones `drl.linalg.exit-lstsq` and `drl.linalg.exit-pca` (3 h each, 6 h); the Factor Risk Lab project `prj.linalg.factor-risk` (8 h); Linear Algebra Lab, 12 drills of 30 minutes (6 h) | 20.0 | 13.3% | Capstones: `1.4.exit` `estHours` (6 of its 11); `prj.linalg.factor-risk` milestone hours (8); four lab sets (`set.elimination.lab`, `set.orthogonality.lab`, `set.eigen.lab`, `set.svd.lab`), kind `practice` (6) |
| **Total** | | **150.0** | 100% | Estimates: **140.5 h (93.7%)**; spent-only time 9.5 h |

**The estimate sum.** Lessons 24.5, level reviews 4.5, practice sets 12.0, homework 36.0, `exm.linalg` 3.0, `1.4.exit` 11.0 (capstones 6, the real final 3, its marking 1, the review of `exm.linalg` 1), companion 35.5, project 8.0 and Lab 6.0 make 140.5 h. The Redo queue (5.5 h), the practice paper (3 h) and its review (1 h) are spent time only: a redo is a second attempt at an item whose first attempt already earned its hours, and a practice paper is assembled on demand from the practice pool (`ASSESSMENT.md` 3.2), so counting either would credit the same hours twice. The pace factor absorbs them (`ASSESSMENT.md` 8.7).

Solving time (everything except the 24.75 hours of watching and reading) is 125.25 hours, 83.5%, above the curriculum's 60% floor (rule 2).

**Pace.** About 14 weeks at the curriculum's 15-hour week. 1.7 Finance runs alongside 1.3 to 1.6 and takes $\frac{100}{820}\approx 12\%$ of the Main block, about 1.1 h a week (ASSESSMENT.md 8.3), so 1.4 has about 7.9 h of Main a week for its lessons, homework, reading and exams (108 h: 13.7 weeks). The Build block (3 h) carries the capstones, the project and the Lab drills (20 h, about 1.5 h a week, leaving room for the weekly commit of code companions); the Review block (2 h) carries practice and the Redo queue (22 h, about 1.6 h a week). Draft 1 said 12 weeks because it left 1.7's share out.

## 2. Courses

### 2.0 Conventions for every lesson below

**Ids** follow CONTENT-FORMAT.md section 4.1: `crs.<course>`, `lvl.<course>.<level>`, `lsn.<course>.<slug>`, `set.<course>.<level>` for homework, `set.<course>.<level>-practice` for practice sets, `set.<course>.lab` for Lab sets, `exm.linalg` for the topic exam, `prj.linalg.<name>` for the project. Course namespaces and display codes: `elimination` (V), `subspaces` (N), `orthogonality` (O), `eigen` (H), `svd` (Z). Libraries use the topic namespace `linalg`: `skl.linalg.*`, `mc.linalg.*`, `gen.linalg.*`, `drl.linalg.*`, `crd.linalg.*`. The `#` column is the lesson's number within 1.4; display numbers are computed from position and never stored.

**Display codes.** V (Vectors, Matrices and Elimination), N (Spaces, Rank and the Four Subspaces; N for nullspace), O (Orthogonality and Least Squares), H (Hidden Directions: Determinants and Eigenvalues), Z (Data Matrices: Positive Definiteness, the SVD and PCA). None clashes with Stage 0 (W, P, E, L, Q, F, S, G, T, C, R, K, B, Y, A), 1.1 and 1.2 (Y, B, A, D, T) or the provisional letters of the 1.3 file (D, U, I, X, M), which is why the obvious M (matrices) and X (data matrix) are not used; open item 2 (section 1.5). Draft 1's namespace line still carried M and X for V and Z; draft 2 corrects it. Level V2 is the second level of course V.

**Every lesson** runs the design research 8.3 sequence: a hook on a canvas, free manipulation, the name for what the learner just did, a ladder of 2 to 4 screens adding one constraint each, the trap, the rule stated as a description of the learner's own steps, a transfer screen where a true quant or ML context exists, the summary card, then a quiz of 3 to 5 items. From the owner's skills, every lesson also carries the "why this exists" frame, one common trap, at least one edge case, one worked example with a decision point where the naive route fails, at least one line the learner derives (a `derive` screen; `leap: naive, fails, pointsTo` wherever the step is a creative one), and "concreteness fades": the first screens use pictures and small integers, the last ones use symbols.

In linear algebra these take particular forms:

- **Manipulation before notation** means dragging vector tips, setting weights with sliders, applying a row operation, spinning a direction, morphing a grid or dropping a perpendicular, before any symbol appears.
- **The worked example's decision point** is usually a choice between two routes that are equal in exact arithmetic and unequal in effort or in floating point: columns or rows, eliminate or expand, invert or solve, normal equations or QR, $A^\top A$ or the SVD.
- **Derive** means completing or ordering the lines of a short proof or derivation, or building a formula from the picture (the projection from $\mathbf e\perp\mathbf a$, the normal equations from $A^\top\mathbf e = \mathbf 0$, the eigenvalue equation from a singular $A - \lambda I$).

**Widgets** use the ids of widgets-calc-linalg.md sections 3 to 6 and 11: primitives `plot-2d`, `plot-3d`, `matrix`, `marks`, `drag-point`, `slider`, `stepper`, `token-tray`, `value-table`, `live-formula`, `choice`, `numeric-input`, `expression-input`, `code-cell`, `sim-runner`; composites `derivation-steps`, `vector-combo`, `projection-lab`, `row-column-picture`, `elimination-lab`, `matrix-multiply`, `transform-grid`, `subspace-tester`, `four-subspaces`, `solution-set`, `basis-change`, `least-squares-fit`, `gram-schmidt`, `determinant-lab`, `eigen-finder`, `linear-dynamics`, `svd-stepper`, `low-rank-approx`, `pca-lab`, `condition-number`, `quadratic-form`, `surface-explorer`, `gradient-compass`, `descent-lab`, `lagrange-explorer`, `series-explorer`. From other catalogues: `predict-output` (programming), `complex-plane` (Stage 0). A mode the catalogue does not yet list is marked [mode to add]; section 1.5 collects them.

**Bertie** is built in from the first lesson, under the gate of AI-TUTOR.md 2.2: chat is open from the first screen with the answer sealed; hints, diagnosis and explain-again open after the lesson's first Check; Why? and the open key after a screen is resolved; nothing during a quiz item until it is graded, and nothing while a paper runs. Every task screen carries `tutor.focus` (required), `tutor.notes`, `tutor.reps` and `tutor.avoid`, and every Why? a `why.rep` (CONTENT-FORMAT.md 3.3 and 3.10); no tutor field may contain a value the screen's check accepts (the leak lint). Hints, show-why steps and Bertie's act lines point at elements as `<widget id>/<kind>:<name>`, using the catalogue's element ids (`combo/vec:a`, `mm/A:row:3`, `deriv/line:L1`), which the compiler checks against each widget's `targets(config)`. He never changes answer state; he may propose a ghost preview of a slider or vector setting, which the learner applies. His face follows BERTIE.md 5.3: `correct` or `notyet` on grading, `surprised` on a trap reveal, `celebrate` at the end of a lesson. Section 7 shows every field in full for the sample lesson.

**Quiz item types.** The letters of CONTENT-FORMAT.md 3.4, which are shorthand in these documents only: N numeric (exact rational or decimal), X expression (a polynomial, a condition on $\mathbf b$, a formula), C single choice, M multi-select, O order the steps of a derivation or proof, S sort or match (vectors into subspaces, matrices to cases), I interval or solution set, P point, direction or pair on a canvas (eigen-directions use `angle-mod-pi`), T predict the output of code, A matrix or vector entry (exact by default; relations such as `echelon-row-equivalent`, `rref`, `orthonormal-columns`), K code with tests, and B, which draft 1 proposed and CONTENT-FORMAT.md revision 2 adopted:

| Letter | Type | Check kind |
|---|---|---|
| B | A basis, a parametric solution set or a subspace, graded by the set it describes, so any valid basis or parametrisation is accepted | `predicate`: `same-subspace` (equal rank of the learner's vectors, the reference and both together, and the right count) or `same-affine-set` (from `solution-set`) |

Long-form items appear in homework and exams, never in quizzes. Single and multiple choice (C and M) stay at or below 40% of each course's quiz items (design research 10); section 2.6 counts them.

**Runtime.** Lesson screens run on both targets with Python never loaded: authored code runs in CI and ships as recorded output, recorded on both CPython 3.11 and Pyodide 314.0.7 (decision 15). Code screens and drills declare `needs`: `[]` or `[numpy]` (decision 10), both shipped in the Artifact, so nothing in 1.4 hands off; a device that cannot load Python shows "Run this one in the web app" and accepts a result code (ASSESSMENT.md 2.5, 14.3). Below 768 px every code screen also offers its Parsons version.

**Level review and mastery.** Every level ends with a Level review of 8 items at difficulty 2 to 4, interleaved, with up to 30% drawn from earlier levels of the same course (ASSESSMENT.md 2.4). Homework follows (section 3). Every quiz item is worth 1 mark; a lesson is Mastered at 75% of its quiz marks on the first completion, so 3 of 4 (or 4 of 5 for the sample lesson) (ASSESSMENT.md decision 26).

**Columns.** Objective is one measurable sentence. Hook is the puzzle the lesson opens with. Trap is the misconception the trap screen is built around; Edge is the fringe case the lesson must show. Where a lesson builds on 0.2, 0.3, 1.1 or 1.3, its row names the lesson it extends, and section 1.0's second table lists them all.

### 2.1 Course V: Vectors, Matrices and Elimination (`crs.elimination`, 3 levels, 18 lessons)

From weighted sums of vectors to solving $n\times n$ systems: the column picture of $A\mathbf x$, elimination with pivots and multipliers, matrix multiplication, inverses, $A = LU$ and $PA = LU$, transposes and permutations, block elimination, what elimination costs and why computers pivot, matrices as linear maps, and the first finance use: replicating a payoff. It starts where 0.3 level T5 stopped ($2\times 2$) and works in exact fractions throughout, except in lesson 16, which exists to show floating point failing.

**Level V1: Combinations and the column picture** (`lvl.elimination.combinations`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `lsn.elimination.linear-combinations` Linear combinations | Find weights $c$, $d$ (and $e$ in 3D) that make $c\mathbf v + d\mathbf w$ hit a target in $\mathbb R^2$ or $\mathbb R^3$, by geometry and by solving two equations, and say whether the weights are unique | A robot can step only along $\mathbf v = (2, 1)$ and $\mathbf w = (1, 3)$, any real amount of each, forwards or back. Reach the flag at $(3, 4)$, then the flag at $(0, 5)$ (weights $(1, 1)$, then $(-1, 2)$) | `vector-combo` (combine, solve, integer lattice game), `numeric-input` | Trap: weights must be positive (a negative weight walks backwards and is allowed). Edge: if $\mathbf w$ is a multiple of $\mathbf v$, most targets are unreachable and each reachable one has infinitely many weight pairs; in $\mathbb R^3$ two vectors never reach every target | N, N, P, C |
| 2 | `lsn.elimination.span` Span: a line, a plane or everything | Describe the span of one, two or three vectors in $\mathbb R^2$ or $\mathbb R^3$ as a point, a line, a plane or the whole space, and produce a vector outside a given span with a check | With $\mathbf v = (1, 2, 3)$ and $\mathbf w = (2, 4, 6)$, can you reach $(1, 0, 0)$? Paint everything you can reach | `vector-combo` (span, 2D then 3D), `plot-3d` | Trap: counting vectors as dimensions ("three vectors span $\mathbb R^3$"). Edge: three vectors in $\mathbb R^3$ lying in one plane; the span of $\mathbf 0$ is one point; every span contains $\mathbf 0$ | C, A, A, M |
| 3 | `lsn.elimination.dot-length-angle` Lengths, angles and Cauchy-Schwarz | Compute lengths, unit vectors and angles in $\mathbb R^n$, prove $\lvert\mathbf v\cdot\mathbf w\rvert \le \lVert\mathbf v\rVert\lVert\mathbf w\rVert$ and the triangle inequality without a picture, and bound the correlation of two series from their correlations with a third | In 0.3 lesson 37 the angle between two arrows came from the cosine rule in a drawn triangle. Two years of daily returns are vectors in $\mathbb R^{500}$, where no triangle can be drawn. Why must $\frac{\mathbf v\cdot\mathbf w}{\lVert\mathbf v\rVert\lVert\mathbf w\rVert}$ still lie between $-1$ and 1? | `projection-lab` (dot), `derivation-steps` (order, with the leap), `numeric-input` | Trap: correlation is transitive ($\rho_{XY} = \rho_{YZ} = 0.6$ still allows $\rho_{XZ}$ as low as $-0.28$, so $X$ and $Z$ can move against each other). Edge: equality holds exactly when one vector is a multiple of the other; $\mathbf 0$ has no direction; 0.3 lesson 37's centring before the cosine is assumed, not retaught | N, N, O, C |
| 4 | `lsn.elimination.matrix-times-vector` $A\mathbf x$ is a combination of columns (section 7) | Compute $A\mathbf x$ as a combination of the columns of $A$ and by rows, choose the faster way for a task, and decide whether $A\mathbf x = \mathbf b$ is solvable by asking whether $\mathbf b$ is a combination of the columns | Fund A carries exposure $(2, 1)$ to (equity, rates) per share and Fund B $(-1, 3)$. You hold 3 of A and 2 of B. What is your total exposure? | `vector-combo` (columns-of-A, span), `matrix-multiply` (column view), `derivation-steps`, `predict-output` | Trap: weighting the rows instead of the columns, which computes $A^\top\mathbf x$. Edge: a $3\times 2$ matrix takes 2 inputs and gives 3 outputs; dependent columns give a nonzero $\mathbf x$ with $A\mathbf x = \mathbf 0$ | A, N, T, M, A |
| 5 | `lsn.elimination.independent-columns` Independent columns and $A = CR$ | Find which columns of a matrix are combinations of earlier columns, keep the others as $C$, write $A = CR$, and write a rank-one matrix as a column times a row | $\begin{pmatrix}1&2&3\\2&4&6\\1&3&4\end{pmatrix}$ has nine numbers. How many columns do you need to rebuild it? (Two: column 3 is column 1 plus column 2.) | `vector-combo` (independence, 3D), `matrix-multiply` (outer), `matrix` | Trap: "no zero column and no two equal columns, so the columns are independent". Edge: a multiplication table is rank one, $\mathbf u\mathbf v^\top$; the zero matrix has rank 0 and an empty $C$ | A, N, A, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 3 bounds an unknown correlation from two known ones, as a risk manager filling a correlation matrix must; lesson 4 totals fund exposures and checks them with NumPy's `@`; lesson 5 shows a table of returns that is close to rank one because one market factor drives it, previewing level Z3.

**Level V2: Elimination** (`lvl.elimination.elimination`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 6 | `lsn.elimination.row-column-pictures` Row and column pictures | Solve $2\times 2$ and $3\times 3$ systems as an intersection of lines or planes and as weights on the columns, and classify a system as having one, no or infinitely many solutions in each picture | $x - 2y = 1$ and $3x + 2y = 11$: find where the lines cross, then the weights that combine $(1, 3)$ and $(-2, 2)$ into $(1, 11)$. Same two numbers? (Yes: $(3, 1)$.) | `row-column-picture` (2D, 3D), `numeric-input` | Trap: in 3D, "no two planes parallel, so they meet in a point" (three planes can share a line or form a prism with no common point; 0.2 lesson 53 listed the cases, this screen tests the belief). Edge: moving $\mathbf b$ shifts the row-picture lines without turning them, while in the column picture it moves one arrow; a system never has exactly two solutions | N, C, P, M |
| 7 | `lsn.elimination.elimination-pivots` Elimination as an algorithm: pivots and multipliers | Reduce a system written only as its augmented matrix $[A\;\mathbf b]$ to upper triangular form by subtracting $\ell_{ij} = (\text{entry to clear})/(\text{pivot})$ times the pivot row, one column at a time, apply each step to $\mathbf b$, and back-substitute | In 0.2 lesson 53 you solved three equations by eliminating letters. Here are four equations as a table of 20 numbers and no letters: rows $(2, 1, 0, 1\mid 2)$, $(4, 3, -1, 4\mid 3)$, $(-2, 2, -2, 6\mid -2)$, $(2, -1, 4, 1\mid 12)$. Solve it (multipliers $2, -1, 1$, then $3, -2$, then $2$; pivots $2, 1, 1, 2$; solution $(1, -1, 2, 1)$) | `elimination-lab` (goal U, then solve; multipliers typed by the learner), `row-column-picture` (3D, linked, on the $3\times 3$ ladder screen) | Trap: the multiplier inverted (pivot over entry) or the right-hand column left unchanged. Edge: the order is fixed (column 1 is cleared completely before column 2), which 0.2's "eliminate any unknown from any pair" never needed and a computer always does; a pivot is never 0 by definition; multipliers can be fractions and stay exact | N, A, N, O |
| 8 | `lsn.elimination.breakdown` When elimination breaks down | Tell a temporary breakdown (a zero in the pivot position with a nonzero entry below it, fixed by a row exchange) from a permanent one (no nonzero entry available: the matrix is singular), and say what each means for the solutions | The first pivot position of $\begin{pmatrix}0&2\\3&4\end{pmatrix}$ holds 0. Is the system doomed? | `elimination-lab` (swap allowed), `row-column-picture` | Trap: announcing "no solution" at the first zero pivot. Edge: a singular system has infinitely many solutions when $\mathbf b$ is right; a zero in the last pivot position with a zero right-hand side | C, A, N, B |
| 9 | `lsn.elimination.multiplication-four-ways` Matrix multiplication four ways | Compute $AB$ by entries, by columns ($A$ times each column of $B$), by rows, and as a sum of outer products; show $(AB)C = A(BC)$ from the column view, and choose the cheaper order of a product | Fill one product four different ways and watch the four answers agree. Which way needs no arithmetic when $B$'s first column is $(0, 1, 0)$? | `matrix-multiply` (entry, column, row, outer; shape puzzle; multiplication counter [mode to add]) | Trap: $(A + B)^2 = A^2 + 2AB + B^2$ (it is $A^2 + AB + BA + B^2$: 0.3 lesson 88's $AB\ne BA$ is where this bites). Edge: a $1\times n$ times an $n\times 1$ is a number and an $n\times 1$ times a $1\times n$ is a rank-one matrix; $(AB)\mathbf x$ costs about $n^3$ multiplications and $A(B\mathbf x)$ about $2n^2$, though they are equal | A, A, C, N |
| 10 | `lsn.elimination.elimination-matrices` Elimination matrices | Write each row operation as left multiplication by an elimination matrix $E_{ij}$, combine the steps as $E = E_{32}E_{31}E_{21}$, and invert a step by changing the sign of its multiplier | Can one matrix do "subtract 2 times row 1 from row 2" to every matrix it multiplies? | `elimination-lab` (operations as matrices), `matrix-multiply` (row view) | Trap: multiplying the steps in the order they were done, $E_{21}E_{31}E_{32}$. Edge: $E$ on the right ($AE$) acts on columns, not rows | A, A, O, N |
| 11 | `lsn.elimination.inverses` Inverses by Gauss-Jordan | Compute $A^{-1}$ by reducing $[A\;I]$ to $[I\;A^{-1}]$, state that $A^{-1}$ exists exactly when elimination finds $n$ pivots, prove $(AB)^{-1} = B^{-1}A^{-1}$ in one line, and explain why solving $A\mathbf x = \mathbf b$ never needs $A^{-1}$ | The steps that turn $A$ into $I$: what do they turn $I$ into? | `elimination-lab` (inverse), `derivation-steps`, `transform-grid` (inverse) | Trap: reading the inverse off the right-hand block after the downward pass, when the left block is still $U$ and the right block is the elimination record $E$, not $A^{-1}$. Edge: for a square matrix a left inverse is automatically a right inverse; a $2\times 3$ matrix has right inverses but no two-sided inverse; 0.3 lesson 92's $2\times 2$ formula is Gauss-Jordan done once in letters | A, A, O, C |
| 12 | `lsn.elimination.lu` $A = LU$: elimination as a factorisation | Factor $A = LU$ by writing each multiplier into $L$, solve $A\mathbf x = \mathbf b$ by forward then back substitution, and count the cost: about $n^3/3$ multiply-adds to factor and $n^2$ per right-hand side | A risk system solves $A\mathbf x = \mathbf b$ for one $A$ and 1,000 scenario vectors $\mathbf b$. Must it eliminate 1,000 times? | `elimination-lab` (LU), `matrix-multiply`, `numeric-input` | Trap: the multipliers written into $L$ with the wrong sign, or $L$ taken to be $E$ (whose entries mix multipliers). Edge: $A = LDU$ with unit diagonals; a symmetric $A$ gives $A = LDL^\top$ | A, N, A, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 12 solves 1,000 risk scenarios from one factorisation; lesson 11 inverts a two-step currency conversion and shows the order of undoing.

**Level V3: Structure, cost and meaning** (`lvl.elimination.structure`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 13 | `lsn.elimination.transposes` Transposes and symmetric matrices | Compute transposes, prove $(AB)^\top = B^\top A^\top$ from $(A\mathbf x)^\top\mathbf y = \mathbf x^\top(A^\top\mathbf y)$, and show that $A^\top A$ and $AA^\top$ are symmetric | A covariance matrix is built as $X^\top X$ from a table of centred returns. Why is it symmetric whatever the data? | `matrix-multiply`, `derivation-steps`, `matrix` | Trap: $(AB)^\top = A^\top B^\top$ (the shapes do not even fit when $A$ is $2\times 3$ and $B$ is $3\times 4$). Edge: $A^\top A$ is $n\times n$ while $AA^\top$ is $m\times m$; the inverse of a symmetric matrix is symmetric | A, O, C, N |
| 14 | `lsn.elimination.permutations` Permutations and $PA = LU$ | Build the permutation matrix for a row reordering, show $P^{-1} = P^\top$, tell $PA$ (rows move) from $AP$ (columns move), and factor $PA = LU$ when exchanges are needed | Predict where $P$ sends each row of $A$, then where $AP$ sends each column | `matrix-multiply` (permutation), `elimination-lab` (PA=LU), `choice` | Trap: $PA$ read as permuting columns. Edge: there are $n!$ permutation matrices of size $n$; doing the exchanges first keeps the multipliers in $L$ below the diagonal | A, A, N, C |
| 15 | `lsn.elimination.block-matrices` Block matrices and the Schur complement | Multiply partitioned matrices block by block, eliminate a whole block at once, and derive the Schur complement $S = D - CA^{-1}B$ of $\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\C&D\end{pmatrix}$ | Treat a $4\times 4$ matrix as a $2\times 2$ matrix whose entries are $2\times 2$ blocks, and clear the bottom-left block as if it were a number | `matrix-multiply` (blocks), `derivation-steps`, `elimination-lab` | Trap: commuting blocks, writing $D - BA^{-1}C$ or $D - A^{-1}BC$. Edge: blocks multiply only when their shapes fit; with $A$ singular the formula fails while the whole matrix may be invertible | A, O, X, C |
| 16 | `lsn.elimination.cost-and-pivoting` The cost of elimination and partial pivoting | Count the work of elimination (about $n^3/3$ multiply-adds) and back substitution (about $n^2/2$), and explain with a $2\times 2$ example why floating-point elimination swaps in the largest available pivot even when the pivot is not zero | Solve $\begin{pmatrix}10^{-20}&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}\mathbf x = \begin{pmatrix}1\\2\end{pmatrix}$ in floating point without a row exchange. The computer reports $x_1 = 0$; the true $x_1$ is almost exactly 1 | `elimination-lab` (floating mode [mode to add]), `code-cell`, `value-table` | Trap: "a nonzero pivot is good enough". Edge: this matrix is perfectly well conditioned, so the failure is the algorithm's, not the problem's; doubling $n$ multiplies the operation count by about 8 (measured times are a poorer guide: a multithreaded BLAS hides much of the growth at these sizes [measured]) | N, C, T, N |
| 17 | `lsn.elimination.linear-maps` Matrices as linear transformations | Test whether a map satisfies $T(c\mathbf v + d\mathbf w) = cT(\mathbf v) + dT(\mathbf w)$, build its matrix from the images of the standard basis vectors between spaces of different dimensions, and compose maps by multiplying in the right order | A drawing program shows 3D points on a flat screen: $\mathbf e_1$ lands at $(1, 0)$, $\mathbf e_2$ at $(0, 1)$ and $\mathbf e_3$ at $(\tfrac12, \tfrac12)$. Where does the cube's far corner $(1, 1, 1)$ land, and what shape is the matrix? ($(\tfrac32, \tfrac32)$; $2\times 3$) | `transform-grid` (transform, compose; a non-square $A$, 3D to the plane [mode to add]), `matrix` | Trap: "$T(\mathbf 0) = \mathbf 0$, so $T$ is linear" ($T(x, y) = (x, y^2)$ fixes the origin and fails $T(2\mathbf v) = 2T(\mathbf v)$). Edge: 0.3 lesson 89's translation is recalled, not retaught; a $2\times 3$ matrix flattens space onto a plane, so some nonzero input lands on $\mathbf 0$; a projection onto a line is linear and has no inverse | A, C, A, M |
| 18 | `lsn.elimination.replication` Replication and state prices | Extend 0.2 lesson 54's two-state replication to three states: price a new payoff by solving for the portfolio of traded assets that replicates it, compute state prices $\mathbf q$ from $P^\top\mathbf q = \mathbf p$ (payoff matrix $P$, prices $\mathbf p$), and recognise an arbitrage when a state price comes out negative | A bond, a stock and a call trade in a world with three possible tomorrows. A new security pays $(0, 1, 3)$. What must it cost? (The lesson's own market, not homework V3's) | `elimination-lab` (solve), `matrix`, `numeric-input` | Trap: pricing by the expected payoff under real-world probabilities. Edge: with fewer assets than states (an incomplete market) state prices are not unique and a payoff has a range of arbitrage-free prices, previewing the nullspace (level N1) | N, N, C, A |

Transfer screens: lesson 13 explains why every covariance matrix is symmetric; lesson 15 reads the Schur complement as the variance left after the best hedge (1.5 proves it); lesson 16 is why `np.linalg.solve` pivots; lesson 18 is the replication argument behind every no-arbitrage price in 1.7.

### 2.2 Course N: Spaces, Rank and the Four Subspaces (`crs.subspaces`, 4 levels, 19 lessons)

The structure behind $A\mathbf x = \mathbf b$: vector spaces and subspaces, the column space and nullspace, reduced row echelon form, rank, the complete solution, independence, basis and dimension, Strang's four fundamental subspaces and their dimensions, $A^\top A$, graphs, and linear maps written in different bases. This is the first course with proofs in every level, and the one most learners find abstract, so every abstraction arrives through a picture (a squashed grid, a shifted solution line, a currency loop) before its definition.

**Level N1: Vector spaces and the nullspace** (`lvl.subspaces.spaces`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 19 | `lsn.subspaces.vector-spaces` Vector spaces beyond arrows | Check the closure rules for sets of vectors, matrices, polynomials and functions, name the zero vector of each, and decide whether a set with its operations is a vector space | Is the set of all $2\times 2$ matrices a space of "vectors"? Is the set of all portfolios whose weights add to 1? | `subspace-tester`, `choice`, `matrix` | Trap: the fully invested portfolios ($\sum w_i = 1$) taken as a vector space (they miss $\mathbf 0$ and are not closed under addition: an affine set). Edge: the zero space $\{\mathbf 0\}$; all functions $\mathbb R\to\mathbb R$; the positive reals with "addition" $xy$ and "scaling" $x^c$ form a vector space whose zero vector is 1 | M, X, N, C |
| 20 | `lsn.subspaces.subspaces` Subspaces: closure and counterexamples | Decide whether a subset of $\mathbb R^2$, $\mathbb R^3$ or a matrix space is a subspace, and produce a counterexample (two vectors whose sum escapes, or a scalar multiple that escapes) when it is not | The two axes of the plane together: find two vectors in that set whose sum escapes it | `subspace-tester` (union-of-lines, quadrant, half-plane, curve) | Trap: the union of two subspaces taken as a subspace. Edge: a line not through the origin; the intersection of subspaces is always a subspace; $\{\mathbf 0\}$ and the whole space count | P, C, M, P |
| 21 | `lsn.subspaces.column-space` The column space $C(A)$ | Describe $C(A)$ as the set of all $A\mathbf x$, decide by elimination whether a given $\mathbf b$ lies in it, and state in which space it lives | For $A = \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\4&3\\2&3\end{pmatrix}$, which $\mathbf b$ can be reached? Paint them. (The plane $2b_1 - b_2 + b_3 = 0$.) | `vector-combo` (span, 3D), `row-column-picture` (3D), `elimination-lab` | Trap: $C(A)$ placed in $\mathbb R^n$, the input space. Edge: $C(A) = \mathbb R^m$ exactly when every row gets a pivot; $C(AB)$ sits inside $C(A)$ | C, X, A, N |
| 22 | `lsn.subspaces.nullspace` The nullspace $N(A)$ | Find every $\mathbf x$ with $A\mathbf x = \mathbf 0$ for small matrices, prove $N(A)$ is a subspace, and read it as the set of inputs the map sends to $\mathbf 0$ | This map squashes the plane onto a line. Which inputs land on $\mathbf 0$? | `transform-grid` (singular), `solution-set`, `vector-combo` | Trap: $N(A)$ placed in $\mathbb R^m$; the solutions of $A\mathbf x = \mathbf b$ with $\mathbf b \ne \mathbf 0$ called a subspace. Edge: $N(A) = \{\mathbf 0\}$ exactly when the columns are independent | B, C, B, N |
| 23 | `lsn.subspaces.rref-special-solutions` Pivots, free variables and special solutions | Reduce $A$ to its reduced row echelon form $R$, identify pivot and free columns, and write one special solution per free variable whose combinations fill $N(A)$ | Reduce $\begin{pmatrix}1&2&2&4\\3&8&6&16\end{pmatrix}$ and give every solution of $A\mathbf x = \mathbf 0$ (special solutions $(-2, 0, 1, 0)$ and $(0, -2, 0, 1)$) | `elimination-lab` (RREF), `solution-set` | Trap: setting every free variable to 0, which finds only $\mathbf x = \mathbf 0$; or copying entries of $R$ into the special solution without changing their sign. Edge: with more unknowns than equations there is always a nonzero solution; a zero column is a free column | A, B, N, O |

Transfer screens: lesson 19 shows fully invested portfolios as a subspace shifted away from the origin; lesson 22 reads the nullspace of a payoff matrix as the portfolios that cost nothing and pay nothing in every state.

**Level N2: Rank, independence and dimension** (`lvl.subspaces.rank`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 24 | `lsn.subspaces.rank` Rank: the true size of a matrix | Compute the rank as the number of pivots, relate it to the numbers of independent columns and rows, and write a rank-$r$ matrix as an $m\times r$ matrix times an $r\times n$ one | The $100\times 100$ multiplication table has 10,000 entries. What is its rank? (1.) | `elimination-lab` (RREF), `matrix-multiply` (outer), `four-subspaces` (dims) | Trap: counting the nonzero rows of $A$ before elimination. Edge: row rank equals column rank (a surprise, proved in level N3); a tiny change to one entry can raise the rank, a warning for floating point | N, N, A, C |
| 25 | `lsn.subspaces.complete-solution` The complete solution $\mathbf x = \mathbf x_p + \mathbf x_n$ | Solve $A\mathbf x = \mathbf b$ completely as one particular solution plus the whole nullspace, and derive the conditions on $\mathbf b$ from the zero rows of the reduced system | Slide the free variable: the solutions trace a line, but not through the origin. Why that line? | `solution-set` (editable $\mathbf b$, 3D), `elimination-lab` | Trap: the solution set called a subspace; two different particular solutions thought to give different solution sets. Edge: an inconsistent row $0 = 1$; $\mathbf x_p$ is not unique, the set is | B, X, C, N |
| 26 | `lsn.subspaces.rank-cases` The four rank cases | Classify an $m\times n$ matrix of rank $r$ as $r = m = n$, $r = n < m$, $r = m < n$ or $r < m$ and $r < n$, and state the number of solutions of $A\mathbf x = \mathbf b$ in each case | Four matrices, four shapes, four answers to "how many solutions?" Match them | `solution-set`, `four-subspaces` (dims), `choice` | Trap: "more equations than unknowns means no solution" and "more unknowns than equations means infinitely many" (both fail when the rank is lower). Edge: full column rank gives 0 or 1 solution, and the 0 case is where least squares begins (level O1) | S, C, N, S |
| 27 | `lsn.subspaces.independence` Linear independence | Test vectors for independence by checking $N(A) = \{\mathbf 0\}$ for the matrix with those columns, and prove that any $n + 1$ vectors in $\mathbb R^n$ are dependent | Drag three vectors in $\mathbb R^3$ until the box they span flattens. At that moment, write one as a combination of the other two | `vector-combo` (independence, 3D), `elimination-lab` | Trap: "no two of them are parallel, so they are independent". Edge: any set containing $\mathbf 0$ is dependent; $1$, $x$ and $x^2$ are independent functions | C, A, N, M |
| 28 | `lsn.subspaces.basis-dimension` Basis and dimension | Find a basis for a column space, a nullspace, or a space of matrices or polynomials, and prove that every basis of a subspace has the same number of vectors | Two different bases for the same plane in $\mathbb R^3$. Must every basis of that plane have two vectors? | `vector-combo` (span), `elimination-lab` (RREF), `derivation-steps` | Trap: the pivot columns of $R$ given as a basis for $C(A)$ (take the pivot columns of $A$). Edge: $3\times 3$ symmetric matrices form a space of dimension 6; polynomials of degree at most 3, dimension 4; $\{\mathbf 0\}$ has dimension 0 and an empty basis | B, N, O, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 24 shows a $60\times 50$ returns matrix of full rank whose "effective" rank is about 3 (previewing level Z3); lesson 26 sets a regression with more features than observations as the $r = m < n$ case.

**Level N3: The four fundamental subspaces** (`lvl.subspaces.four`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 29 | `lsn.subspaces.row-space-left-nullspace` The row space and the left nullspace | Find a basis for $C(A^\top)$ from the nonzero rows of $R$ and for $N(A^\top)$ from the elimination record $EA = R$, and read the left nullspace as the combinations of equations that give $0 = 0$ | Which combinations of these three equations collapse to $0 = 0$? They are exactly the conditions on $\mathbf b$ | `elimination-lab` (record $E$), `four-subspaces` (sort) | Trap: the column space of $R$ taken as the column space of $A$ (elimination keeps the row space and changes the column space). Edge: $N(A^\top) = \{\mathbf 0\}$ exactly when every row has a pivot | B, B, C, X |
| 30 | `lsn.subspaces.fundamental-theorem` The big picture: $r$, $n - r$, $r$, $m - r$ | State and use the dimensions of the four fundamental subspaces, prove $\dim C(A) + \dim N(A) = n$ from pivot and free counts, and place given vectors in the right subspace | Sort these vectors into the four boxes, then fill in the four dimensions | `four-subspaces` (sort, dims, geometric) | Trap: $\dim N(A) = m - r$. Edge: $A$ and $A^\top$ have the same rank; an invertible matrix has two zero subspaces | S, N, N, C |
| 31 | `lsn.subspaces.rank-of-products` $A^\top A$ and the rank of products | Prove $N(A^\top A) = N(A)$, conclude that $A^\top A$ is invertible exactly when $A$ has independent columns, and show $\operatorname{rank}(AB) \le \min(\operatorname{rank}A, \operatorname{rank}B)$ | A regression has "trade size in pounds" and "trade size in pence" as two features. It runs with no error, yet a laptop and the app report different coefficients on the two (one even of the opposite sign) and the same fitted values. Which matrix is singular, and what do both machines agree on? | `derivation-steps` (leap: $\mathbf x^\top A^\top A\mathbf x = \lVert A\mathbf x\rVert^2$), `four-subspaces`, `matrix` | Trap: "$A^\top A$ is always invertible". Edge: $\operatorname{rank}(A^\top A) = \operatorname{rank}(A)$ though $A^\top A$ and $AA^\top$ differ in size; in floating point this $A^\top A$ is only nearly singular, so NumPy raises nothing on either runtime and the split between the two coefficients is rounding noise [measured] | O, C, N, A |
| 32 | `lsn.subspaces.graphs-incidence` Graphs, incidence matrices and arbitrage | Build the incidence matrix of a directed graph, interpret its nullspace (constant potentials), column space (potential differences) and left nullspace (loops), and check nodes $-$ edges $+$ loops $= 1$ for a connected graph | Four currencies, five quoted rates. Is there a loop of trades that makes money? | `four-subspaces` (graph mode [mode to add]), `matrix`, `numeric-input` | Trap: looking for loops in $N(A)$ instead of $N(A^\top)$. Edge: a disconnected graph has a two-dimensional nullspace; a tree has no loops, so every set of rates on a tree is arbitrage-free | A, N, B, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 31 is the dummy-variable failure in miniature, with the two machines' outputs recorded side by side; lesson 32 tests triangular arbitrage as a loop condition on log rates.

**Level N4: Bases, coordinates and linear transformations** (`lvl.subspaces.transformations`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 33 | `lsn.subspaces.coordinates` Coordinates in a basis | Find the coordinates of a vector in a non-standard basis by solving $B\mathbf c = \mathbf x$, convert back with $\mathbf x = B\mathbf c$, and read coordinates in an orthonormal basis as dot products | Read a point's coordinates on a skewed grid | `basis-change` (coordinates), `numeric-input` | Trap: multiplying by $B$ where $B^{-1}$ is needed. Edge: coordinates exist and are unique only for a basis; in an orthonormal basis $c_i = \mathbf q_i^\top\mathbf x$ | N, N, C, A |
| 34 | `lsn.subspaces.matrix-of-a-map` The matrix of a linear transformation | Build the matrix of a linear map between spaces with chosen bases, including differentiation and integration of polynomials, and compute with it | Write $\frac{d}{dx}$ acting on cubics $a + bx + cx^2 + dx^3$ as a $4\times 4$ matrix | `matrix` (primitive screen, widget catalogue section 8), `matrix-multiply` | Trap: images placed in rows; a matrix built in one basis applied to coordinates in another. Edge: $\frac{d}{dx}$ has the constants as nullspace and no inverse; integrate then differentiate is the identity, the other order is not | A, A, N, C |
| 35 | `lsn.subspaces.change-of-basis` Change of basis and similar matrices | Compute $B^{-1}AB$, the matrix of the same map in a new basis, and recognise similar matrices as one map described in two bases | $A = \begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}$ looks like a tangle. In the basis $(1, 1)$, $(1, -1)$ it is "stretch by 3, stretch by 1" | `basis-change` (matrix-of-T), `transform-grid` | Trap: $BAB^{-1}$ where $B^{-1}AB$ is meant. Edge: similar matrices share trace, determinant, rank and (level H2) eigenvalues; no basis makes a shear diagonal | A, C, N, A |
| 36 | `lsn.subspaces.kernel-image` Kernel, image and invertibility | For a linear map $T: V\to W$, compute the dimensions of its kernel and image, prove $T$ is one-to-one exactly when its kernel is $\{\mathbf 0\}$, and prove that for $V = W$ of finite dimension one-to-one and onto are equivalent | Can a linear map from $\mathbb R^3$ to $\mathbb R^2$ be one-to-one? Can one from $\mathbb R^2$ to $\mathbb R^3$ be onto? | `derivation-steps`, `four-subspaces`, `choice` | Trap: assuming one-to-one gives onto between spaces of different dimensions. Edge: on all polynomials, $\frac{d}{dx}$ is onto but not one-to-one and "multiply by $x$" is one-to-one but not onto, so the equivalence needs finite dimension | C, N, O, N |
| 37 | `lsn.subspaces.factor-neutral` Factor exposures and neutral portfolios | Compute a portfolio's factor exposures $B^\top\mathbf w$, find every portfolio with zero exposure to chosen factors and zero net cost as a nullspace, and pick one by a normalisation | Four stocks, a market factor and a tech factor. Build a portfolio with no exposure to either that costs nothing | `solution-set`, `elimination-lab` (RREF), `matrix` | Trap: "neutral" taken to mean equal weights, or neutral to one factor only; the zero-cost constraint forgotten. Edge: with as many independent constraints as stocks, only $\mathbf w = \mathbf 0$ is neutral | A, B, N, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 35 rotates a two-factor model into uncorrelated factors (the same factors in a new basis); lesson 37 builds a market- and sector-neutral book.

### 2.3 Course O: Orthogonality and Least Squares (`crs.orthogonality`, 3 levels, 16 lessons)

What to do when $A\mathbf x = \mathbf b$ has no solution, which is the normal state of data: project $\mathbf b$ onto the column space. Orthogonal subspaces, projections onto lines and subspaces, the normal equations, least squares as both geometry and calculus, then least squares as a quant uses it (betas, multi-factor regressions, collinearity, weights, ridge) and as a computer should do it (QR, Householder). It ends on orthogonal functions, where the same projection gives Fourier series.

**Level O1: Orthogonality and projections** (`lvl.orthogonality.projections`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 38 | `lsn.orthogonality.orthogonal-subspaces` Orthogonal subspaces and complements | Show that $N(A)$ is orthogonal to $C(A^\top)$ and that they are orthogonal complements in $\mathbb R^n$, and split any $\mathbf x$ into a row-space part and a nullspace part | Every vector in this nullspace is perpendicular to every row. Coincidence? | `four-subspaces` (geometric), `projection-lab` | Trap: two walls of a room taken as orthogonal subspaces (they share a line). Edge: complements have dimensions adding to $n$; $A\mathbf x$ depends only on the row-space part of $\mathbf x$ | C, N, A, B |
| 39 | `lsn.orthogonality.project-line` Projection onto a line | Derive $\mathbf p = \frac{\mathbf a^\top\mathbf b}{\mathbf a^\top\mathbf a}\mathbf a$ from the condition $\mathbf e\perp\mathbf a$, build the projection matrix $P = \frac{\mathbf a\mathbf a^\top}{\mathbf a^\top\mathbf a}$, and verify $P^2 = P = P^\top$ | 0.3 lesson 38 projected one vector onto another.
