Now find one matrix that projects every vector of the plane onto the line through $\mathbf a = (3, 1)$, and use it on $(2, 4)$ ($P = \frac{1}{10}\begin{pmatrix}9&3\\3&1\end{pmatrix}$; $(3, 1)$) | `projection-lab` (predict, onto-line, `showMatrix`), `derivation-steps` | Trap: dividing by $\lVert\mathbf a\rVert$ instead of $\mathbf a^\top\mathbf a$ (right only for a unit vector; here it gives $\sqrt{10}\,(3, 1)$), or writing $P = \frac{\mathbf a^\top\mathbf a}{\mathbf a\mathbf a^\top}$. Edge: projecting twice changes nothing; rescaling $\mathbf a$ leaves $P$ unchanged; $P$ has rank 1 | P, N, A, O |
| 40 | `lsn.orthogonality.project-subspace` Projection onto a subspace: the normal equations | Derive $A^\top A\hat{\mathbf x} = A^\top\mathbf b$ from $\mathbf e\perp C(A)$, compute $\mathbf p = A\hat{\mathbf x}$ and $P = A(A^\top A)^{-1}A^\top$, and show $I - P$ projects onto $N(A^\top)$ | $\mathbf b$ hangs above the plane of two columns. Find the point $\mathbf p$ on the plane so that $\mathbf e = \mathbf b - \mathbf p$ meets both columns at right angles | `projection-lab` (onto-plane), `derivation-steps`, `matrix` | Trap: "cancelling" $A(A^\top A)^{-1}A^\top$ to $I$ (valid only for square invertible $A$). Edge: projecting onto a plane given by its normal, $P = I - \frac{\mathbf n\mathbf n^\top}{\mathbf n^\top\mathbf n}$ | A, A, C, N |
| 41 | `lsn.orthogonality.least-squares` Least squares: the best line | Fit $C + Dt$ to data by solving the normal equations, and show that minimising $\lVert A\mathbf x - \mathbf b\rVert^2$ by calculus (1.3 lesson 122) and projecting $\mathbf b$ onto $C(A)$ give the same equations | Drag a line through $(0, 6)$, $(1, 0)$, $(2, 0)$ to make the total area of the squares on the vertical misses as small as you can (best line $5 - 3t$, least total 6) | `least-squares-fit` (residuals, squares, error map), `derivation-steps` | Trap: minimising the sum of residuals (many lines make it zero) or the perpendicular distances. Edge: with an intercept the residuals add to zero; an outlier counts through its square | N, N, P, C |
| 42 | `lsn.orthogonality.least-squares-geometry` The picture in $\mathbb R^m$: fitting more than lines | Set up least squares for polynomials and several regressors, read $\mathbf b$, $\mathbf p$ and $\mathbf e$ as vectors in $\mathbb R^m$, and prove that the residual is orthogonal to every column, so that with an intercept the residuals sum to zero and the fitted values average to $\bar b$ | With three data points, see $\mathbf b$, $\mathbf p$ and $\mathbf e$ in $\mathbb R^3$: the best line is a projection onto a plane | `least-squares-fit` (r3, degree), `projection-lab` (onto-plane) | Trap: "an extra column can make the fit worse" (the residual never grows). Edge: a perfect fit happens exactly when $\mathbf b\in C(A)$; a degree $m - 1$ polynomial through $m$ points fits perfectly and predicts badly | A, C, N, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 41 fits a trend line to a short price series and states what least squares assumes about the misses; lesson 42 reads "the residuals are orthogonal to the market" as the reason a CAPM residual has zero sample correlation with the market.

**Level O2: Least squares in practice** (`lvl.orthogonality.regression`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 43 | `lsn.orthogonality.capm-beta` CAPM beta as a projection | Estimate a stock's alpha and beta by regressing its excess returns on the market's with the normal equations, and read $\beta$ as the projection coefficient $\frac{\mathbf m_c^\top\mathbf s_c}{\mathbf m_c^\top\mathbf m_c}$ of the centred stock vector on the centred market vector, which is 1.3 lesson 122's $\operatorname{cov}/\operatorname{var}$ seen as geometry | Twelve months of returns for a stock and the market. How much does the stock move for each 1% the market moves? | `least-squares-fit` (capm), `projection-lab` (onto-line), `code-cell` | Trap: regressing the market on the stock and inverting the slope. Edge: $\beta = \rho\,\sigma_s/\sigma_m$, so a high-beta stock can have a low correlation; residuals are uncorrelated with the market by construction | N, N, K, C |
| 44 | `lsn.orthogonality.multi-factor-fwl` Several regressors and the Frisch-Waugh-Lovell theorem | Fit a multi-factor regression, and prove that the coefficient on one regressor equals the slope of the residualised response on the residualised regressor | Does a momentum factor explain a fund's returns once the market is accounted for? The simple slope and the multiple-regression coefficient disagree. Which answers the question? | `least-squares-fit` (matrices), `derivation-steps`, `code-cell` | Trap: multiple-regression coefficients read as simple-regression slopes. Edge: with orthogonal regressors the two coincide; "partialling out" is projecting with $I - P$ | N, O, C, K |
| 45 | `lsn.orthogonality.collinearity` Collinearity and the dummy-variable trap | Detect a rank-deficient design matrix by its rank before solving, describe all least-squares solutions as $\hat{\mathbf x}_p + N(A)$, and repair the model by dropping or combining columns | A model of stock returns has an intercept and one dummy column for each of 11 sectors. `solve` on the normal equations prints coefficients without complaint, and the same code on another machine prints different ones [measured]. Why? | `solution-set`, `least-squares-fit` (matrices), `matrix`, `predict-output` (both runtimes' recorded outputs) | Trap: the dummy-variable trap (the dummies add up to the intercept column). Edge: no runtime raises here, because rounding leaves $A^\top A$ nearly singular rather than singular; an exactly singular matrix raises `LinAlgError` on CPython and returns `nan` in the app (1.1 lesson 67); so test `np.linalg.matrix_rank` or the condition number first (lesson 88) | C, N, B, A |
| 46 | `lsn.orthogonality.weighted-ls` Weighted least squares and outliers | Solve $A^\top WA\hat{\mathbf x} = A^\top W\mathbf b$ with weights set to the reciprocals of known noise variances, and measure how far one outlier moves an ordinary least-squares fit | Drag one data point far from the others. How far does the best line follow it? | `least-squares-fit` (outlier, movable points), `numeric-input` | Trap: equal weights when noise variances differ, or weights equal to the variances instead of their reciprocals. Edge: a zero weight deletes a point; weighted least squares is ordinary least squares on rows scaled by $\sqrt{w_i}$ | N, C, N, A |
| 47 | `lsn.orthogonality.ridge` Ridge regression | Derive ridge regression $(A^\top A + \lambda I)\hat{\mathbf x} = A^\top\mathbf b$ as least squares with a penalty, prove it has exactly one solution for every $\lambda > 0$, and trace the coefficients as $\lambda$ grows | Ten observations, twelve features: infinitely many coefficient vectors fit perfectly. Which one should we report? | `least-squares-fit` (ridge mode [mode to add]), `slider`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: penalising unscaled features (the answer then depends on units) or the intercept; expecting ridge to set coefficients exactly to zero (that is the lasso, in 2.1). Edge: $\lambda\to 0$ gives the minimum-norm least-squares solution, $\lambda\to\infty$ gives $\mathbf 0$; ridge is least squares on $A$ stacked over $\sqrt\lambda I$ | X, N, C, O |
| 48 | `lsn.orthogonality.ls-in-numpy` Least squares in code: when the normal equations lose digits | Implement least squares by the normal equations in NumPy, compare with `np.linalg.lstsq`, and explain from $\kappa(A^\top A) = \kappa(A)^2$ why the normal equations lose about twice as many digits | Fit a degree-10 polynomial to 50 points on $[0, 1]$ whose true coefficients are all 1. The normal equations miss by about $10^{-3}$ to $10^{-2}$; `lstsq` and QR by about $10^{-9}$ | `code-cell`, `predict-output`, `value-table` | Trap: "mathematically equal, so numerically equal". Edge: this Vandermonde matrix has condition number about $2\times 10^7$, so $A^\top A$ has about $4\times 10^{14}$; the digits lost are noise, which is why CPython misses by $2.1\times 10^{-3}$ and the app by $2.4\times 10^{-2}$ [measured]; centring and scaling $t$ helps | K, N, T, C |

Transfer screens: every lesson in O2 is a transfer lesson: lesson 43 (beta), 44 (factor attribution), 45 (sector models), 46 (heteroscedastic noise, as in returns of different volatility), 47 (a 12-feature return predictor), 48 (the first half of the exit capstone in miniature).

**Level O3: Orthonormal bases and QR** (`lvl.orthogonality.qr`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 49 | `lsn.orthogonality.orthonormal` Orthonormal columns and orthogonal matrices | Verify $Q^\top Q = I$, show that $Q$ keeps lengths and angles, project with $QQ^\top$, and recognise rotations, reflections and permutations as orthogonal matrices | Which $2\times 2$ matrices never change the length of any vector? | `transform-grid`, `projection-lab`, `matrix` | Trap: $QQ^\top = I$ for a tall $Q$ (only $Q^\top Q = I$). Edge: a square $Q$ has $Q^{-1} = Q^\top$; projection onto orthonormal columns needs no inverse | C, A, N, A |
| 50 | `lsn.orthogonality.gram-schmidt` Gram-Schmidt | Turn independent vectors into an orthonormal basis of the same space by subtracting projections onto the earlier directions and normalising, predicting each new direction before it is computed | Predict where the next orthogonal vector points before the widget draws it | `gram-schmidt` (predict, 2D then 3D) | Trap: subtracting only the projection onto the first vector, or forgetting to normalise. Edge: a dependent input produces the zero vector, which exposes the dependence; reordering the inputs changes the basis, not the space | P, A, N, O |
| 51 | `lsn.orthogonality.qr` $A = QR$ and least squares by QR | Assemble $A = QR$ from Gram-Schmidt with $R = Q^\top A$ upper triangular, and solve least squares from $R\hat{\mathbf x} = Q^\top\mathbf b$ | With orthonormal columns the normal equations collapse into a triangular system. Watch it happen | `gram-schmidt` (showQR), `derivation-steps`, `matrix` | Trap: $R$ taken as lower triangular; the reduced $Q$ ($m\times n$) confused with a square one. Edge: a zero on $R$'s diagonal flags dependent columns; the residual norm is $\sqrt{\lVert\mathbf b\rVert^2 - \lVert Q^\top\mathbf b\rVert^2}$ without computing the residual | A, A, N, O |
| 52 | `lsn.orthogonality.householder` Stable orthogonalisation: modified Gram-Schmidt and Householder | Build a Householder reflection $H = I - 2\mathbf u\mathbf u^\top$ that sends a vector onto a coordinate axis, and explain why classical Gram-Schmidt loses orthogonality in floating point while modified Gram-Schmidt and Householder keep it | Run classical Gram-Schmidt on three nearly parallel vectors in floating point. Two of the "orthonormal" vectors have a dot product of 0.5 | `code-cell`, `projection-lab`, `value-table` | Trap: "algorithms equal in exact arithmetic behave the same on a computer". Edge: $H$ is symmetric and orthogonal with $H^2 = I$; the sign of $\mathbf u$ is chosen to avoid subtracting nearly equal numbers | A, T, C, N |
| 53 | `lsn.orthogonality.function-spaces` Orthogonal functions: Fourier series and Legendre polynomials | Compute inner products of functions on an interval, find Fourier coefficients as projections, and orthogonalise $1, x, x^2$ on $[-1, 1]$ | Approximate a square wave by sines. Each coefficient is a projection: compute the first ($\tfrac{4}{\pi}$) | `series-explorer` (Fourier mode [mode to add]), `derivation-steps`, `plot-2d` | Trap: assuming $1, x, x^2$ are orthogonal ($1$ and $x^2$ are not, on $[-1, 1]$). Edge: the space is infinite-dimensional; Parseval's identity is Pythagoras with infinitely many terms; partial sums overshoot near the jump (Gibbs) | N, X, C, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 51 refits the CAPM regression of lesson 43 by QR and gets the same beta; lesson 52 explains what `np.linalg.qr` does inside (Householder, through LAPACK's `geqrf` [recalled]); lesson 53 previews seasonality models in 2.2.

### 2.4 Course H: Hidden Directions: Determinants and Eigenvalues (`crs.eigen`, 4 levels, 23 lessons)

Some directions a matrix only stretches. Finding them turns powers, Markov chains, differential equations and covariance matrices into one-dimensional problems. The course opens with determinants because $\det(A - \lambda I) = 0$ is how eigenvalues are found by hand, then builds eigenvalues from the picture (spin a vector until $A\mathbf x$ lines up), diagonalisation, dynamics (Markov, PageRank, $e^{At}$, vector autoregressions, Jordan), and the symmetric and complex cases (the spectral theorem, Rayleigh quotients, Hermitian and Fourier matrices).

**Level H1: Determinants** (`lvl.eigen.determinants`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 54 | `lsn.eigen.det-volume` The determinant as signed volume | Compute $3\times 3$ determinants as the signed volume of the box the columns span, read the sign as orientation by the right-hand rule, and say what a zero means for the columns (0.3 lesson 91's $2\times 2$ area factor is the starting point) | Columns $(1, 0, 1)$ and $(0, 2, 1)$ are fixed; drag the third column $(1, 1, h)$ up and down. At what height does the box flatten, and what happens to the sign as you pass it? ($\det = 2h - 3$: flat at $h = \tfrac32$, and the sign changes there) | `determinant-lab` (area, `dims: 3`), `transform-grid` (determinant, 3D) | Trap: "no two columns are parallel, so the determinant is not zero" ($(1, 0, 1)$, $(0, 2, 1)$ and $(1, 2, 2)$ are pairwise non-parallel and coplanar). Edge: a rotation has determinant 1 and a reflection in a plane $-1$; swapping two columns flips the sign and keeps the volume | N, C, N, A |
| 55 | `lsn.eigen.det-properties` Three rules define the determinant | Derive from three rules ($\det I = 1$; a row exchange flips the sign; linearity in each row separately) that equal rows give 0, that subtracting a multiple of one row from another changes nothing, and that a triangular determinant is the product of its diagonal | Shear, swap, scale: predict each change in area before applying it | `determinant-lab` (properties), `derivation-steps` | Trap: $\det(A + B) = \det A + \det B$; $\det(2A) = 2\det A$ (it is $2^n\det A$). Edge: $\det A^\top = \det A$, so every rule holds for columns too | N, C, O, N |
| 56 | `lsn.eigen.det-product-pivots` The product rule and the product of pivots | Compute a determinant as $\pm$ the product of the pivots, use $\det(AB) = \det A\det B$ to get $\det A^{-1} = 1/\det A$, and compare elimination's $n^3/3$ operations with the big formula's $n!$ terms | A $10\times 10$ determinant by the big formula has 3,628,800 terms; elimination needs about 333 multiplications. How? | `elimination-lab` (determinant from pivots), `determinant-lab` (product) | Trap: $\det A^{-1} = -\det A$; the sign of the row exchanges forgotten. Edge: an orthogonal matrix has determinant $\pm 1$; a permutation matrix's determinant is its sign | N, N, C, N |
| 57 | `lsn.eigen.cofactors` The big formula and cofactor expansion | Expand a $3\times 3$ or $4\times 4$ determinant along the row or column with most zeros, derive the big formula as a sum over permutations, and set up recurrences for banded matrices | Expand along the row with the most zeros. How many of the 24 terms of this $4\times 4$ survive? | `determinant-lab` (cofactor), `stepper` | Trap: the checkerboard signs dropped. Edge: the second-difference matrix (2 on the diagonal, $-1$ beside it) has determinant $n + 1$; a zero row gives 0 from any expansion | N, N, X, C |
| 58 | `lsn.eigen.cramer-inverse` Cramer's rule, the inverse formula and volume | Derive $A^{-1} = C^\top/\det A$ and Cramer's rule $x_j = \det B_j/\det A$, use them to see how $A^{-1}$ depends on the entries, and say why neither is used to compute | Solve a $2\times 2$ system as a ratio of two parallelogram areas | `determinant-lab` (cramer), `derivation-steps` | Trap: using Cramer's rule to solve a large system. Edge: an integer matrix with determinant $\pm 1$ has an integer inverse; a tetrahedron's volume is $\tfrac16\lvert\det\rvert$ | N, A, O, N |
| 59 | `lsn.eigen.det-densities` Determinants in change of variables and densities | Use $\lvert\det A\rvert$ to transform areas and probability densities under $\mathbf y = A\mathbf x$, and read $\det\Sigma$ as the generalised variance in the multivariate normal density | Points spread evenly over the unit square are pushed through $A$. The density on the image is no longer 1. What is it? | `transform-grid` (determinant, nonlinear), `sim-runner`, `numeric-input` | Trap: multiplying the density by $\lvert\det A\rvert$ (it divides). Edge: a singular $A$ squashes the square onto a segment, where no density exists; the Jacobian of 1.3 lesson 134 is the local version | N, C, N, X |

Transfer screens: lesson 56 computes a log-determinant from pivots, as likelihood code does; lesson 59 reads $\det\Sigma$ in the multivariate normal density that 1.5 introduces.

**Level H2: Eigenvalues and eigenvectors** (`lvl.eigen.eigenvalues`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 60 | `lsn.eigen.directions-that-stay` Directions that do not turn | Find the eigen-directions of a $2\times 2$ matrix by searching for $\mathbf x$ with $A\mathbf x$ on the same line, and verify $A\mathbf x = \lambda\mathbf x$ algebraically | Spin $\mathbf x$ round the circle until $A\mathbf x$ lies on the same line as $\mathbf x$ | `eigen-finder` (find-directions), `transform-grid` | Trap: a direction that $A$ reverses ($\lambda < 0$) rejected. Edge: $\mathbf 0$ is never an eigenvector but $\lambda = 0$ is allowed (singular $A$); a projection's eigenvalues are 1 and 0 | P, N, C, A |
| 61 | `lsn.eigen.characteristic-polynomial` $\det(A - \lambda I) = 0$ | Find eigenvalues as roots of $\det(A - \lambda I)$ and eigenvectors as $N(A - \lambda I)$, motivated by needing $A - \lambda I$ to have a nonzero nullspace | Slide $\lambda$ and watch $A - \lambda I$ act on the circle. At two values the image collapses to a segment | `eigen-finder` (lambda-shift), `derivation-steps` (leap: naive "solve $A\mathbf x = \lambda\mathbf x$ for two unknowns", fails "the unknowns multiply", points to "for fixed $\lambda$ it is a nullspace question") | Trap: eigenvalues read off the diagonal of a non-triangular matrix; eigenvalues of $A + B$ taken as sums. Edge: a triangular matrix has its eigenvalues on the diagonal; row operations change eigenvalues | X, N, B, C |
| 62 | `lsn.eigen.trace-determinant` Trace, determinant and a $2\times 2$ shortcut | Use $\lambda_1 + \lambda_2 = \operatorname{tr}A$ and $\lambda_1\lambda_2 = \det A$ to find and check eigenvalues, including $\lambda = m\pm\sqrt{m^2 - p}$ with $m$ the mean and $p$ the product | $\begin{pmatrix}8&4\\2&6\end{pmatrix}$: trace 14, determinant 40. Name the eigenvalues in your head (10 and 4) | `eigen-finder` (trace-det), `numeric-input` | Trap: trusting a computed pair without checking its sum and product. Edge: a reflection has eigenvalues 1 and $-1$; the trace and determinant fix the eigenvalues, not the matrix | N, N, N, C |
| 63 | `lsn.eigen.complex-repeated` Complex and repeated eigenvalues | Compute the complex eigenvalues of rotation-like matrices and read off the angle and scale, and identify matrices with a repeated eigenvalue but too few eigenvectors | Rotate the plane by $90^\circ$. No direction survives. Where did the eigenvalues go? | `eigen-finder` (classify), `transform-grid`, `complex-plane` | Trap: a repeated eigenvalue assumed to bring two independent eigenvectors (the identity against a shear). Edge: complex eigenvalues of a real matrix come in conjugate pairs; $\begin{pmatrix}a&-b\\b&a\end{pmatrix}$ has eigenvalues $a\pm ib$ (0.3 level T6) | X, C, N, B |
| 64 | `lsn.eigen.diagonalisation` Diagonalisation $A = X\Lambda X^{-1}$ | Diagonalise a matrix with $n$ independent eigenvectors, prove that eigenvectors for distinct eigenvalues are independent, and say which matrices cannot be diagonalised | On the eigenvector grid, $A$ only stretches. Switch grids and watch | `basis-change` (diagonalise), `eigen-finder`, `matrix` | Trap: $\Lambda$'s order not matching the columns of $X$; every matrix assumed diagonalisable. Edge: a shear is not diagonalisable; distinct eigenvalues guarantee diagonalisability, repeated ones do not forbid it | A, A, C, O |
| 65 | `lsn.eigen.powers-fibonacci` Powers, difference equations and Fibonacci | Compute $A^k\mathbf x_0$ by expanding $\mathbf x_0$ in eigenvectors, derive Binet's formula, and predict long-run growth from the largest $\lvert\lambda\rvert$ | $F_{100}$ without computing 99 steps? ($F_{100} = 354{,}224{,}848{,}179{,}261{,}915{,}075$) | `linear-dynamics` (discrete, decomposition), `numeric-input` | Trap: $(X\Lambda X^{-1})^k = X^k\Lambda^kX^{-k}$. Edge: components with $\lvert\lambda\rvert < 1$ die out, so $F_{k+1}/F_k$ tends to the golden ratio; a negative eigenvalue makes its component alternate in sign | N, X, N, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 62 is the interview habit (eigenvalues of a $2\times 2$ in the head, checked by trace and determinant); lesson 65 compounds a two-state population of investors.

**Level H3: Dynamics** (`lvl.eigen.dynamics`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 66 | `lsn.eigen.markov` Markov matrices and steady states | Find the steady state of a Markov matrix as its eigenvector for $\lambda = 1$, explain why the other eigenvalues satisfy $\lvert\lambda\rvert\le 1$, and convert between Strang's column convention and the row convention of 1.5 | Markets switch between bull and bear regimes: a bull day is followed by a bull day 95% of the time, a bear day by a bear day 80% of the time. Iterate until the shares stop changing ($(0.8, 0.2)$; second eigenvalue 0.75) | `linear-dynamics` (markov), `eigen-finder` | Trap: the two conventions mixed, using $P$ where $P^\top$ is needed. Edge: the swap matrix $\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ has eigenvalue $-1$ and never settles; a chain with two closed classes has two steady states | P, N, C, N |
| 67 | `lsn.eigen.power-method-pagerank` The power method and PageRank | Compute a dominant eigenvector by repeated multiplication and normalisation, estimate the convergence rate $\lvert\lambda_2/\lambda_1\rvert$, and build the damped PageRank matrix | Rank four web pages by how often a random surfer visits them | `linear-dynamics` (markov), `code-cell`, `value-table` | Trap: assuming convergence from every start and for every matrix (it fails when $\lvert\lambda_1\rvert = \lvert\lambda_2\rvert$ or when the start has no component along the top eigenvector). Edge: damping 0.85 makes every entry positive, which guarantees one steady state | N, C, K, N |
| 68 | `lsn.eigen.differential-equations` $\frac{d\mathbf u}{dt} = A\mathbf u$ | Solve linear systems of differential equations as $\mathbf u(t) = \sum c_ie^{\lambda_it}\mathbf x_i$, and classify the origin as a stable or unstable node, a saddle, a spiral or a centre from the eigenvalues | Drop starting points into the flow. Which ones run into the origin? | `linear-dynamics` (continuous, field), `eigen-finder` | Trap: the discrete test $\lvert\lambda\rvert < 1$ used for a continuous system (the test is $\operatorname{Re}\lambda < 0$). Edge: purely imaginary eigenvalues give closed orbits; a zero eigenvalue gives a line of equilibria | C, X, S, N |
| 69 | `lsn.eigen.matrix-exponential` The matrix exponential $e^{At}$ | Define $e^{At}$ by its power series (1.3 lessons 99 to 101), compute it as $Xe^{\Lambda t}X^{-1}$ by diagonalisation, and compute it directly for a nilpotent matrix where diagonalisation is impossible | For $A = \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ the series for $e^{At}$ stops after two terms. What is it? | `derivation-steps`, `linear-dynamics` (continuous), `matrix` | Trap: $e^{A + B} = e^Ae^B$ (true when $AB = BA$). Edge: $e^{At}$ is always invertible, with inverse $e^{-At}$; the exponential of a rotation generator is a rotation | A, A, C, N |
| 70 | `lsn.eigen.var-stability` Vector autoregressions and stability | Decide whether $\mathbf x_{t+1} = A\mathbf x_t + \mathbf c + \boldsymbol\varepsilon_t$ is stable from the eigenvalues of $A$, compute its long-run mean $(I - A)^{-1}\mathbf c$ and its impulse responses as powers of $A$ | Two interest rates feed each other through $A = \begin{pmatrix}0.6&0.5\\0.5&0.6\end{pmatrix}$. Every entry is below 1. Do shocks die out? (No: one eigenvalue is 1.1.) | `linear-dynamics` (discrete), `sim-runner`, `plot-2d` | Trap: judging stability by the entries or the diagonal. Edge: an eigenvalue exactly 1 is a unit root: shocks never fade, yet a combination of the series can still be stable (cointegration, 2.2) | N, C, A, N |
| 71 | `lsn.eigen.similar-jordan` Similar matrices and the Jordan form | Decide whether two matrices are similar from trace, determinant, eigenvalues and the number of independent eigenvectors, and state the Jordan form of a defective $2\times 2$ or $3\times 3$ matrix and its powers | $\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\0&2\end{pmatrix}$ and $\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\0&2\end{pmatrix}$ have the same eigenvalues. Are they one map in two bases? | `basis-change` (matrix-of-T), `linear-dynamics`, `choice` | Trap: "same eigenvalues means similar". Edge: a Jordan block's powers grow like $k\lambda^{k-1}$, so a defective matrix with $\lvert\lambda\rvert < 1$ can grow before it decays; $\lambda I$ is similar only to itself | C, A, N, X |

Transfer screens: lesson 66 is a two-regime market model and the conversion that 1.5 needs; lesson 67 ranks a network of counterparties by the same method as PageRank; lesson 70 is the stability check every vector autoregression in 2.2 needs.

**Level H4: Symmetric and complex matrices** (`lvl.eigen.symmetric`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 72 | `lsn.eigen.spectral-theorem` Symmetric matrices: the spectral theorem | Prove that a real symmetric matrix has real eigenvalues and that its eigenvectors for distinct eigenvalues are orthogonal, and write $S = Q\Lambda Q^\top$ | Turn a circle into an ellipse with a symmetric matrix. Its axes always meet at right angles. Try to break that | `svd-stepper` (spectral), `eigen-finder`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: assuming every matrix has orthogonal eigenvectors. Edge: a repeated eigenvalue of a symmetric matrix still comes with a full set of eigenvectors, and any orthonormal basis of the eigenspace works | C, O, A, N |
| 73 | `lsn.eigen.spectral-sum` $S = \sum\lambda_i\mathbf q_i\mathbf q_i^\top$ and functions of a matrix | Write a symmetric matrix as a weighted sum of rank-one projections, and compute $S^k$, $S^{-1}$ and $S^{1/2}$ from it | Build a $2\times 2$ symmetric matrix from two perpendicular directions and two stretch factors | `svd-stepper` (spectral), `matrix-multiply` (outer) | Trap: a non-unit $\mathbf q$ in $\lambda\mathbf q\mathbf q^\top$. Edge: $S^{1/2}$ needs nonnegative eigenvalues; $S^{-1}$ keeps the eigenvectors and inverts the eigenvalues | A, A, N, C |
| 74 | `lsn.eigen.rayleigh` The Rayleigh quotient | Show that $\frac{\mathbf x^\top S\mathbf x}{\mathbf x^\top\mathbf x}$ is largest at the top eigenvector and smallest at the bottom one, and apply it to the direction of greatest variance | Of all unit-length weight vectors on two assets, which gives the largest variance $\mathbf w^\top\Sigma\mathbf w$? | `quadratic-form`, `eigen-finder`, `pca-lab` (variance) | Trap: maximising $\mathbf x^\top S\mathbf x$ with no constraint (unbounded), or confusing the unit-length constraint with the budget constraint $\sum w_i = 1$. Edge: the minimum-variance unit vector can be long-short | N, P, C, N |
| 75 | `lsn.eigen.complex-hermitian` Complex vectors, Hermitian and unitary matrices | Compute complex lengths and inner products with conjugates, recognise Hermitian ($S^{\mathsf H} = S$) and unitary ($U^{\mathsf H}U = I$) matrices, and show Hermitian eigenvalues are real | The vector $(1, i)$ has "length squared" $1 + i^2 = 0$. Something is wrong | `derivation-steps`, `matrix`, `numeric-input` | Trap: forgetting the conjugate. Edge: a rotation matrix is unitary with eigenvalues $e^{\pm i\theta}$; real symmetric matrices are Hermitian | N, C, X, A |
| 76 | `lsn.eigen.fourier-matrix` The Fourier matrix and the FFT | Build the $n\times n$ Fourier matrix from the roots of unity, show its columns are orthogonal, invert it, and explain how splitting into even and odd parts gives the FFT's $\tfrac12 n\log_2 n$ multiplications | Multiplying two polynomials of degree 1,000 term by term takes about a million multiplications. Three FFTs of length 2,048 take about 36,000. How? | `matrix`, `derivation-steps`, `complex-plane` | Trap: $F^{-1} = F^\top$ (it is $\bar F/n$). Edge: every circulant matrix has the Fourier vectors as eigenvectors, which is why convolution becomes multiplication | A, N, C, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 74 finds the riskiest unit-length long-short portfolio; lesson 76 shows why option pricing by Fourier methods and fast convolution both rest on the FFT (named, not taught).

### 2.5 Course Z: Data Matrices: Positive Definiteness, the SVD and PCA (`crs.svd`, 4 levels, 22 lessons)

The course where linear algebra meets data. Positive definite matrices (energy, covariance, Cholesky, the minimum-variance portfolio, gradients of quadratic forms), the singular value decomposition (geometry, computation, the four subspaces, low-rank approximation, the pseudoinverse, conditioning), principal component analysis done properly (centring, scaling, explained variance, yield curves, equity returns and their noise floor, NumPy), and four lessons on matrices in learning and risk (shrinkage, gradient descent, graph Laplacians, how eigenvalues are computed). It ends the item at the exit capstones.

**Level Z1: Positive definite matrices** (`lvl.svd.positive-definite`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 77 | `lsn.svd.energy-tests` Positive definite: five tests that agree | Test a symmetric matrix for positive definiteness by its eigenvalues, pivots, upper-left determinants, the energy $\mathbf x^\top S\mathbf x > 0$, and $S = A^\top A$ with independent columns, and prove the energy test from the eigenvalue test | For $S = \begin{pmatrix}2&b\\b&2\end{pmatrix}$, raise $b$ until the bowl $z = \mathbf x^\top S\mathbf x$ turns into a saddle. At what $b$? (2.) | `quadratic-form`, `elimination-lab` (pivots), `surface-explorer` | Trap: "all entries positive, so positive definite" ($\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\2&1\end{pmatrix}$ is not), or "positive diagonal, so positive definite". Edge: semidefinite on the boundary $b = 2$; one negative diagonal entry rules it out at once | M, N, C, N |
| 78 | `lsn.svd.quadratic-forms` Quadratic forms, ellipses and minima | Rotate a quadratic form to its principal axes, sketch $\mathbf x^\top S\mathbf x = 1$ with semi-axes $1/\sqrt{\lambda_i}$ along the eigenvectors, and classify a critical point from its Hessian (1.3 lessons 119 and 120) | Which way does the ellipse $x^2 + xy + y^2 = 1$ lean, and how long are its axes? (Long axis $\sqrt2$ along $(1, -1)$; short axis $\sqrt{2/3}$ along $(1, 1)$.) | `quadratic-form`, `svd-stepper` (spectral), `surface-explorer` | Trap: semi-axes $\sqrt{\lambda_i}$ instead of $1/\sqrt{\lambda_i}$. Edge: an indefinite form gives a hyperbola, a semidefinite one a pair of parallel lines | P, N, C, N |
| 79 | `lsn.svd.covariance-matrices` Covariance and correlation matrices | Build a sample covariance matrix $\frac{1}{n-1}X_c^\top X_c$ from centred data, prove it is positive semidefinite, convert it to a correlation matrix with $D^{-1/2}SD^{-1/2}$, and test whether a proposed correlation matrix is valid | Three assets with $\rho_{12} = 0.9$, $\rho_{13} = 0.9$ and $\rho_{23} = -0.9$. Could that be real? (No: it has an eigenvalue of $-0.8$.) | `quadratic-form` (correlation-3), `matrix`, `code-cell` | Trap: any symmetric matrix with unit diagonal and entries in $[-1, 1]$ taken as a correlation matrix. Edge: with fewer observations than assets the sample covariance is singular; a zero eigenvalue is an exact linear relation (a perfect hedge); the code screen tests $\lambda_{\min}$ with `np.linalg.eigvalsh`, because `np.linalg.cholesky` of this matrix raises on CPython and returns `nan` in the app [measured] | N, C, A, N |
| 80 | `lsn.svd.portfolio-variance` Portfolio variance and the minimum-variance portfolio | Compute $\mathbf w^\top\Sigma\mathbf w$, derive the minimum-variance weights $\mathbf w = \Sigma^{-1}\mathbf 1/(\mathbf 1^\top\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf 1)$ with a Lagrange multiplier (1.3 lesson 128 found it for uncorrelated assets), and compute them by solving $\Sigma\mathbf y = \mathbf 1$ instead of inverting | Two assets with volatilities 20% and 30% and correlation 0.5. What mix has the least risk? ($\tfrac67$ in the first.) | `lagrange-explorer` (simplex), `quadratic-form`, `code-cell` | Trap: inverting $\Sigma$ explicitly; believing the weights must be positive. Edge: with correlation near 1 the weights explode into large long-short positions; perfectly correlated assets make $\Sigma$ singular | N, N, X, C |
| 81 | `lsn.svd.cholesky` Cholesky factorisation and correlated simulation | Factor a positive definite matrix as $S = LL^\top$ by symmetric elimination, turn independent standard normals $\mathbf z$ into normals $L\mathbf z$ with covariance $S$, and use Cholesky's failure as a test for positive definiteness | You can simulate independent standard normals. How do you make two with correlation 0.8? | `elimination-lab` (symmetric), `sim-runner`, `plot-2d` | Trap: multiplying by $\Sigma$ instead of $L$ (the covariance becomes $\Sigma^2$), or using $L^\top$ on column vectors. Edge: for $2\times 2$, $L = \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\\rho&\sqrt{1-\rho^2}\end{pmatrix}$; $L$ is unique once its diagonal is positive; the failure shows as an exception on CPython and as `nan` entries in the app, while a hand-written Cholesky raises on both (homework Z1's drill) | A, N, C, K |
| 82 | `lsn.svd.matrix-calculus` Gradients of linear and quadratic forms | Derive $\nabla(\mathbf a^\top\mathbf x) = \mathbf a$, $\nabla(\mathbf x^\top S\mathbf x) = (S + S^\top)\mathbf x$ and $\nabla\lVert A\mathbf x - \mathbf b\rVert^2 = 2A^\top(A\mathbf x - \mathbf b)$, recover the normal equations and ridge by setting gradients to zero, and check a gradient by finite differences | The normal equations took a page of geometry in lesson 40. Get them in one line from calculus | `derivation-steps`, `gradient-compass`, `code-cell` | Trap: $\nabla(\mathbf x^\top S\mathbf x) = S\mathbf x$ (the factor 2 for symmetric $S$ missing). Edge: for non-symmetric $S$ only the symmetric part $\tfrac12(S + S^\top)$ matters; the Hessian of $\lVert A\mathbf x - \mathbf b\rVert^2$ is $2A^\top A$, semidefinite, so the critical point is a minimum | X, X, C, K |

Transfer screens: lesson 79 checks a correlation matrix a risk system was handed; lesson 80 is the minimum-variance portfolio; lesson 81 simulates two correlated assets for a Monte Carlo pricer (1.7, 2.3).

**Level Z2: The singular value decomposition** (`lvl.svd.svd`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 83 | `lsn.svd.svd-geometry` Rotate, stretch, rotate | Read the singular values and singular directions of a $2\times 2$ matrix from the image of the unit circle, and build a matrix with a given image ellipse | Every $2\times 2$ matrix sends the unit circle to an ellipse. Find the input directions that land on its axes | `svd-stepper` (svd, build), `eigen-finder` (ellipse) | Trap: singular values taken as the absolute eigenvalues (true for symmetric matrices; the shear $\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ has eigenvalues 1, 1 and singular values 1.618 and 0.618). Edge: rank one collapses the ellipse to a segment; an orthogonal matrix keeps the circle | N, P, C, N |
| 84 | `lsn.svd.computing-svd` Computing the SVD from $A^\top A$ | Compute $\sigma_i^2$ and $\mathbf v_i$ as the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of $A^\top A$, then $\mathbf u_i = A\mathbf v_i/\sigma_i$, and explain why computing $U$ separately from $AA^\top$ can break $A = U\Sigma V^\top$ | Find the SVD of $\begin{pmatrix}3&0\\4&5\end{pmatrix}$ by hand ($\sigma_1 = 3\sqrt5$, $\sigma_2 = \sqrt5$) | `svd-stepper`, `derivation-steps`, `matrix` | Trap: signs of independently computed $\mathbf u_i$ and $\mathbf v_i$ that do not match. Edge: zero singular values need $\mathbf u_i$ from $N(A^\top)$; for a tall matrix $A^\top A$ is the small, cheap one | N, A, A, C |
| 85 | `lsn.svd.svd-bases` The SVD and the four subspaces | Read orthonormal bases of all four fundamental subspaces from the full SVD, write $A = \sum_{i=1}^r\sigma_i\mathbf u_i\mathbf v_i^\top$, and tell the full SVD from the reduced one | Level N3's big picture drawn again, now with perpendicular axes that $A$ maps onto each other | `four-subspaces`, `svd-stepper`, `matrix-multiply` (outer) | Trap: the shapes of the full and reduced forms confused ($U$ is $m\times m$ or $m\times r$). Edge: the rank is the number of nonzero singular values; $A\mathbf v_i = \sigma_i\mathbf u_i$ and $A^\top\mathbf u_i = \sigma_i\mathbf v_i$ | S, N, B, C |
| 86 | `lsn.svd.eckart-young` Low-rank approximation | Approximate a matrix by its first $k$ singular layers, compute the error $\lVert A - A_k\rVert_F^2 = \sum_{i>k}\sigma_i^2$, and choose $k$ for a target share of the energy | Rebuild a 64 by 64 image from layers. How many layers keep 90% of the energy? | `low-rank-approx` (image, layers, noise) | Trap: keeping the largest entries instead of the largest singular values. Edge: equal singular values make the best rank-$k$ approximation non-unique; noise adds a floor of small singular values | N, N, C, N |
| 87 | `lsn.svd.pseudoinverse` Left, right and pseudoinverses | Compute the left inverse $(A^\top A)^{-1}A^\top$ and the right inverse $A^\top(AA^\top)^{-1}$ when they exist and the pseudoinverse $A^+ = V\Sigma^+U^\top$ always, and show that $A^+\mathbf b$ is the shortest least-squares solution | A rank-one matrix has no inverse. Can anything map its column space back onto its row space? | `svd-stepper` (pseudoinverse), `four-subspaces`, `solution-set` | Trap: $A^+ = (A^\top A)^{-1}A^\top$ used when $A$ has dependent columns. Edge: $A^+$ is the limit of ridge as $\lambda\to 0$; $(A^+)^+ = A$ | A, A, C, N |
| 88 | `lsn.svd.norms-conditioning` Matrix norms and the condition number | Compute $\lVert A\rVert_2 = \sigma_1$, $\lVert A\rVert_F$ and $\kappa(A) = \sigma_1/\sigma_n$, and bound how much a relative error in $\mathbf b$ can grow in $\mathbf x$ | For $\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&1.0001\end{pmatrix}$, nudge $\mathbf b$ by 0.01% and the solution can move by up to 400% ($\kappa\approx 40{,}000$) | `condition-number` (sensitivity, norm-balls), `svd-stepper` | Trap: a small determinant read as ill-conditioning ($0.1I$ has determinant $10^{-n}$ and $\kappa = 1$). Edge: $\kappa(A^\top A) = \kappa(A)^2$, lesson 48's lost digits now proved; orthogonal matrices have $\kappa = 1$ | N, N, C, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 86 compresses a matrix of returns to a few factors; lesson 88 explains why minimum-variance weights from an inverted covariance matrix jump from day to day.

**Level Z3: Principal component analysis** (`lvl.svd.pca`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 89 | `lsn.svd.pca-direction` The direction of greatest variance | Find the first principal direction of 2D data as the top eigenvector of its covariance matrix $S$ and as the top right singular vector of the centred data matrix, and show the two routes agree | Spin a line through the cloud's centre to make the spread of the projected points as large as possible | `pca-lab` (find-direction, variance) | Trap: forgetting to centre. Edge: a round cloud has no preferred direction (equal eigenvalues); the variance along the best line equals $\lambda_1$ | P, N, C, N |
| 90 | `lsn.svd.pca-explained-variance` Explained variance, scree plots and reconstruction | Compute explained-variance ratios $\sigma_i^2/\sum_j\sigma_j^2$, choose the number of components by a stated rule, and reconstruct data from $k$ components with its error | Ten features, ten bars. Where would you cut? | `pca-lab` (scree, reconstruct), `low-rank-approx` | Trap: ratios computed from $\sigma_i$ instead of $\sigma_i^2$. Edge: the reconstruction error equals the discarded variance; a clear "knee" may not exist | N, N, C, N |
| 91 | `lsn.svd.pca-scaling` Centring, scaling and what PCA is not | Decide whether to standardise before PCA, show that PCA's line minimises perpendicular distances while regression minimises vertical ones, and handle the sign of components | Record one feature in pence instead of pounds and watch the first component swing onto it | `pca-lab` (standardise toggle), `least-squares-fit` | Trap: taking the first principal direction to be the regression line. Edge: PCA of the correlation matrix is PCA of standardised data; a component and its negative are the same component | C, N, P, N |
| 92 | `lsn.svd.yield-curve-pca` PCA on yield-curve changes: level, slope and curvature | Name the shapes of the first three loading vectors of daily yield-curve changes, explain why PCA is applied to changes and not to levels, and hedge a bond portfolio against the first two components | Three loading curves across maturities from 3 months to 30 years. Name them | `pca-lab` (loadings, preset yield-curve-changes), `token-tray` | Trap: reading meaning into a loading's sign (it can flip). Edge: three components usually explain most of the variance of curve changes (Litterman and Scheinkman 1991 report well over 90% [check figure against the paper and the shipped data]); PCA on levels finds trends, not risk factors | S, N, C, N |
| 93 | `lsn.svd.equity-pca-noise` PCA of stock returns: the market factor and the noise floor | Show that the first principal component of a returns correlation matrix is close to an equal-weighted market portfolio, and compare the eigenvalues with the Marchenko-Pastur upper edge $(1 + \sqrt{p/n})^2$ for pure noise | 100 stocks, 500 days. How many eigenvalues of the correlation matrix carry signal? | `pca-lab` (scree; returns-panel preset [mode to add]), `sim-runner`, `low-rank-approx` (generate) | Trap: treating every eigenvalue above 1 as signal. Edge: pure noise with $p/n = 0.2$ spreads eigenvalues from about 0.31 to 2.09; with $p \ge n$ at least $p - n + 1$ eigenvalues of the sample correlation matrix are zero | N, C, N, M |
| 94 | `lsn.svd.pca-numpy` PCA in NumPy from the SVD | Implement PCA with centring and `np.linalg.svd`, match the eigen-decomposition of $S$ up to sign, and fix a sign convention | Your components and the library's disagree in sign. Who is wrong? | `code-cell`, `predict-output`, `pca-lab` | Trap: `np.linalg.eigh` returns eigenvalues in ascending order, so its first column is the smallest component. Edge: `full_matrices=False` for tall data; scores are $U\Sigma$ and loadings are the columns of $V$ | K, T, C, N |

Transfer screens: every lesson in Z3 is a transfer lesson; lesson 92 hedges a bond book; lesson 93 is the starting point of statistical arbitrage on residuals (Avellaneda and Lee 2010, Stage 3 reading); lesson 94 is the second exit capstone in miniature.

**Level Z4: Matrices in learning and risk** (`lvl.svd.learning`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 95 | `lsn.svd.shrinkage` Estimating covariance: noise and shrinkage | Show by simulation that sample eigenvalues spread wider than the true ones, apply linear shrinkage $\hat\Sigma = (1 - \delta)S + \delta\mu I$, and watch the minimum-variance weights settle | The true covariance of 20 assets is the identity. From 50 days of data, the sample eigenvalues run from about 0.16 to 2.4. Which portfolio does the optimiser now favour? | `sim-runner`, `pca-lab` (scree), `code-cell` | Trap: sample eigenvalues taken as unbiased (the largest is biased up and the smallest down). Edge: shrinkage keeps the eigenvectors and pulls the eigenvalues towards their mean; with $n < p$ it is the only way to invert | N, C, N, K |
| 96 | `lsn.svd.gradient-descent-conditioning` Gradient descent on a quadratic: why conditioning sets the speed | Analyse gradient descent on $f(\mathbf x) = \tfrac12\mathbf x^\top S\mathbf x - \mathbf b^\top\mathbf x$ in the eigenbasis, show each error component shrinks by $1 - \eta\lambda_i$ per step, and find the best step $\eta = 2/(\lambda_{\min} + \lambda_{\max})$ with rate $(\kappa - 1)/(\kappa + 1)$ | In 1.3 lesson 117 you found by trial that gradient descent on $x^2 + 10y^2$ blows up for steps a little above 0.1. Predict that limit and the fastest step from the matrix alone (limit $\eta < \tfrac{1}{10}$; best $\eta = \tfrac{1}{11}$, shrinking the error by $\tfrac{9}{11}$ per step) | `descent-lab`, `quadratic-form`, `eigen-finder` | Trap: "a bigger step is always faster" (above $2/\lambda_{\max}$ it diverges). Edge: feature scaling in 2.1 is preconditioning; $\kappa = 1$ converges in one step | N, N, C, X |
| 97 | `lsn.svd.graph-laplacian` Graph Laplacians and spectral clustering | Build the Laplacian $L = D - W = B^\top B$ of a graph, show it is positive semidefinite with the constant vector in its nullspace, and split a graph by the signs of the eigenvector of the second-smallest eigenvalue | Six stocks and a network of strong correlations. Split them into two groups cutting the fewest strong links | `four-subspaces` (graph mode [mode to add]), `eigen-finder`, `matrix` | Trap: using the eigenvector of the smallest eigenvalue (it is constant and splits nothing). Edge: the number of zero eigenvalues counts the connected components; the second eigenvalue measures how well connected the graph is | A, N, S, C |
| 98 | `lsn.svd.eigen-algorithms` How computers find eigenvalues | Run steps of the QR algorithm on a small symmetric matrix and watch it become diagonal, and explain why software never finds eigenvalues as roots of the characteristic polynomial | Change one coefficient of $(x-1)(x-2)\cdots(x-20)$ by $2^{-23}$ and watch its roots scatter into the complex plane (Wilkinson's polynomial) | `code-cell`, `value-table`, `stepper` | Trap: computing eigenvalues as polynomial roots. Edge: shifts make the QR algorithm converge fast; the power method of lesson 67 is the QR algorithm run on one vector | T, C, N, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 95 is how every desk's covariance estimate is regularised (Ledoit and Wolf 2004); lesson 96 is why features are scaled before training (2.1); lesson 97 clusters stocks by correlation.
